Kernel Thinning
\name Raaz Dwivedi \email dwivedi@cornell.edu, \name Lester Mackey \email lmackey@microsoft.com

Abstract
We introduce kernel thinning, a new procedure for compressing a distribution ℙ \mathbb{P} more effectively than i.i.d. sampling or standard thinning. Given a suitable reproducing kernel 𝐤 ⋆ \mathbf{k}_{\star} and 𝒪 ⁡ ( n 2 ) \mathcal{O}(n^{2}) time, kernel thinning compresses an n n -point approximation to ℙ \mathbb{P} into a n \sqrt{n} -point approximation with comparable worst-case integration error across the associated reproducing kernel Hilbert space. The maximum discrepancy in integration error is 𝒪 d ( n − 1 / 2 log ⁡ n ) \mathcal{O}_{d}(n^{-1/2}\sqrt{\log n}) in probability for compactly supported ℙ \mathbb{P} and 𝒪 d ​ ( n − 1 2 ​ ( log ⁡ n ) ( d + 1 ) / 2 ​ log ⁡ log ⁡ n ) \mathcal{O}_{d}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\log n)^{(d+1)/2}\sqrt{\log\log n}) for sub-exponential ℙ \mathbb{P} on ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} . In contrast, an equal-sized i.i.d. sample from ℙ \mathbb{P} suffers Ω ( n − 1 / 4 ) \Omega(n^{-1/4}) integration error. Our sub-exponential guarantees resemble the classical quasi-Monte Carlo error rates for uniform ℙ \mathbb{P} on [ 0 , 1 ] d [0,1]^{d} but apply to general distributions on ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} and a wide range of common kernels. Moreover, the same construction delivers near-optimal L ∞ L^{\infty} coresets in 𝒪 ⁡ ( n 2 ) \mathcal{O}(n^{2}) time. We use our results to derive explicit non-asymptotic maximum mean discrepancy bounds for Gaussian, Matérn, and B-spline kernels and present two vignettes illustrating the practical benefits of kernel thinning over i.i.d. sampling and standard Markov chain Monte Carlo thinning, in dimensions d = 2 d=2 through 100 100 .



Keywords:
coresets, distribution compression, Markov chain Monte Carlo, maximum mean discrepancy, reproducing kernel Hilbert space, thinning
1
1

1

Accepted for presentation as an extended abstract at the Conference on Learning Theory (COLT) 2021.

1
Introduction

Monte Carlo and Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) methods
(
Brooks et al. 2011
)
are commonly used to approximate intractable target expectations
\mathbb{P}f\triangleq{\mathbb{E}}_{X\sim\mathbb{P}}[f(X)]
of
\mathbb{P}
-integrable functions
f
with asymptotically exact averages
\mathbb{P}_{n}f\!\triangleq\!\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i})

based on points
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
generated from a Markov chain.
A standard practice, to minimize the expense of downstream function evaluation, is to
thin
the Markov chain output down to a smaller size
n_{\mathrm{out}}
by keeping only every
({n/}{n_{\mathrm{out}}})
-th sample point
(
Owen 2017
)
. We call this approach
standard thinning
, and such sample compression is critical in fields like computational cardiology in which each function evaluation triggers an organ or tissue simulation consuming thousands of CPU hours
(
Niederer et al. 2011
;
Augustin et al. 2016
;
Strocchi et al. 2020
)
.
Unfortunately,
standard thinning also leads to a significant reduction in accuracy. For example, thinning one’s chain down to
n_{\mathrm{out}}=\sqrt{n}
sample points increases integration error from

\mathcal{O}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})
in probability to
\Omega(n^{-\frac{1}{4}})
by the Markov chain central limit theorem
(
Roberts and Rosenthal 2004
, Prop. 29)
.
Our primary contribution is a more effective thinning strategy, which provides
o_{p}(n^{-\frac{1}{4}})
-integration error when
n^{\frac{1}{2}}
points are returned.

1.1
Thinned MMD coresets

We focus on integration error in a reproducing kernel Hilbert space
(RKHS,
Steinwart and Christmann 2008
, Def. 4.18)

of bounded, measurable functions with a target kernel
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}:\real^{d}\times\real^{d}\to\real
for
d\in\mathbb{N}
and RKHS norm
\|{\cdot}\|_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}
.

Assumption 1

(RKHS of bounded, measurable functions)
.

The RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{k}}
of a kernel
\mathbf{k}:\real^{d}\times\real^{d}\to\real
contains only bounded measurable functions.
Equivalently,
\mathbf{k}
is bounded with
\mathbf{k}(x,\cdot)
measurable for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(
Steinwart and Christmann 2008
, Lems. 4.23, 4.24)
.
2
2

2

Throughout, we use
\mathbf{k}
for statements involving a generic kernel that is potentially distinct from the target kernel
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
.

The worst-case integration error
over the RKHS unit ball is given by the kernel
maximum mean discrepancy

(
Gretton et al. 2012
, MMD,)
.

Definition 1

(Maximum mean discrepancy
(
Gretton et al. 2012
)
)
.

For a kernel
\mathbf{k}
satisfying
1
,
we define the kernel
maximum mean discrepancy
,

\displaystyle\mmd_{\mathbf{k}}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle\triangleq\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{k}}:\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{f}}}\right\|_{\mathbf{k}}\leq 1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mu f-\nu f}}\right|\quad\text{for all probability measures $\mu,\nu$ on $\mathbb{R}^{d}$.}\quad

(2)

For sequences of points
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with empirical distributions
\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathbb{Q}^{\prime}
, we overload this notation to write
\mmd_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{S})\triangleq\mmd_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})
and
\mmd_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\triangleq\mmd_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{Q}^{\prime})
.

Given
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
satisfying
1
, a target distribution
\mathbb{P}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and a sequence of

\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued points
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{n}

generated to approximate
\mathbb{P}
, our aim is to identify a
thinned MMD coreset
, a shorter subsequence that continues to approximate
\mathbb{P}
well in
\mmd_{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
.

Definition 2

(MMD coreset)
.

We call a sequence of
n_{\mathrm{out}}
points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with empirical measure
\mathbb{Q}
an
(n_{\mathrm{out}},\varepsilon)
-MMD coreset
for
({\mathbf{k}_{\star}},\mathbb{P})
if
\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})\leq\varepsilon
.

Notably, when the initial sequence is drawn i.i.d. or from a fast-mixing Markov chain targeting
\mathbb{P}
, standard thinning down to size
n_{\mathrm{out}}=n^{\frac{1}{2}}
yields an order
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},n^{-\frac{1}{4}})
-MMD coreset in probability (see
Proposition

1
).
A benchmark for improvement is provided by the online Haar strategy of
Dwivedi et al. 2019
, which generates an
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},\mathcal{O}_{d}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log^{2d}n))
-MMD coreset in probability from
2n^{\frac{1}{2}}
i.i.d. sample points when
\mathbb{P}
is specifically the uniform distribution on the unit cube
[0,1]^{d}
.
3
3

3

Dwivedi et al. 2019
specifically control the
star discrepancy
, a quantity which in turn upper bounds a Sobolev space MMD called the
L^{2}
discrepancy

(
Hickernell 1998
;
Novak and Wozniakowski 2010
)
.

Our goal is to develop thinned coresets of improved quality for any target
\mathbb{P}
with sufficiently fast tail decay.

1.2
Our contributions

To this end, we introduce
kernel thinning
(
Algorithm

1
), a new, practical solution to the thinned MMD coreset problem that takes as input an
(n,\mathcal{O}_{p}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}))
-MMD coreset and outputs an
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},o_{p}(n^{-\frac{1}{4}}))
-MMD coreset for a wide-range of
({\mathbf{k}_{\star}},\mathbb{P})
.
Kernel thinning uses non-uniform randomness and evaluations of a less smooth
square-root kernel

{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
(see
Definition

5
) to partition the input into subsets of comparable quality and then greedily refines the best of these subsets using
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
.
Our primary contributions include:

1.

Better-than-i.i.d. MMD coresets:

Given
n
input points sampled i.i.d. or from a fast-mixing Markov chain,
kernel thinning yields,
in probability,
an

(n^{\frac{1}{2}},\mathcal{O}_{d}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{\log n}))
-MMD coreset for
\mathbb{P}
and
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
with bounded support, an
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},\mathcal{O}_{d}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{\log^{d+1}n\log\log n}))
-MMD coreset for
\mathbb{P}
and
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
with light tails, and an
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},\mathcal{O}_{d}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{d}{2\rho}}\sqrt{\log n\log\log n}))
-MMD coreset for
\mathbb{P}
and
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}^{2}
with
\rho>2d
moments (
Theorems

1
and
1
).
For compactly supported or light-tailed
\mathbb{P}
and
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
, these results compare favorably with
known
\Omega_{d}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})
lower bounds (see
Section

8.1
).
Our guarantees extend to more general input point sequences, including deterministic sequences based on quadrature or kernel herding
(
Chen et al. 2010
)
, and give rise to explicit, non-asymptotic error bounds for a wide variety of popular kernels including Gaussian, Matérn, and B-spline kernels.
While
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},\mathcal{O}_{d}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log^{\frac{d-1}{2}}n))
-MMD coresets have been developed for specific
({\mathbf{k}_{\star}},\mathbb{P})
pairings like the uniform distribution on
[0,1]^{d}
and an
L^{2}
discrepancy kernel
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
(see
Section

8.1
), to the best of our knowledge, no prior
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},o_{p}(n^{-\frac{1}{4}}))
-MMD coreset constructions were known for the range of
\mathbb{P}
and
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
studied in this work.

2.

MMD error from square-root
L^{\infty}
error:

To derive our MMD guarantees for kernel thinning, we first establish an important link between MMD coresets for
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
and
L^{\infty}
coresets
for
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
.

Definition 3

(
L^{\infty}
coreset)
.

For any kernel
\mathbf{k}
satisfying
1
, probability measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
\mu\mathbf{k}(z)\triangleq\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mu}[\mathbf{k}(X,z)]
.
We call a sequence of

n_{\mathrm{out}}
points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with empirical measure
\mathbb{Q}

an
(n_{\mathrm{out}},\varepsilon)
-
L^{\infty}
coreset
for
(\mathbf{k},\mathbb{P})
if
\|\mathbb{P}\mathbf{k}-\mathbb{Q}\mathbf{k}\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon
.

Theorems

2
and
2
show that
any

L^{\infty}
coreset for
({\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}},\mathbb{P})
is also an MMD coreset for
({\mathbf{k}_{\star}},\mathbb{P})
with quality depending on the tail decay of
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
and
\mathbb{P}
.

3.

Online vector balancing in Hilbert spaces:
As a building block for constructing high-quality coresets, we introduce and analyze a Hilbert space generalization of the self-balancing walk of
Alweiss et al. 2021
to partition a sequence of functions (like
({\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}(x_{i},\cdot))_{i=1}^{n}
) into nearly equal halves. Our analysis of this
self-balancing Hilbert walk
(SBHW,
Algorithm

5
) in
Theorem

3
may be of independent interest for solving the online vector balancing problem of
Spencer 1977
in Hilbert spaces (
Corollary

4
).

4.

Efficient, near-optimal
L^{\infty}
coresets:

We then design a symmetrized version of SBHW for RKHSes—kernel halving—that delivers
2
-thinned coresets with small
L^{\infty}
error (
Algorithms

3
and
4
).
The first stage of kernel thinning,
kt-split
, recursively applies kernel halving to
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
to obtain near-minimax-optimal
L^{\infty}
coresets in
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
time with
\mathcal{O}(n\min(d,n))
space (
Corollaries

5
and
6
).

After describing our kernel and input point requirements in
Section

2
, we detail the kernel thinning and kernel halving algorithms in
Section

3
.

Section

4
houses our main MMD guarantees, both for kernel thinning and for generic
L^{\infty}
square-root kernel coresets.
We introduce and analyze the self-balancing Hilbert walk in
Section

5
and present our main
L^{\infty}
guarantees for kernel halving and
kt-split
in
Section

6
.

Section

7
complements our theoretical contributions with two vignettes illustrating the practical benefits of kernel thinning over (a) i.i.d. sampling in dimensions
d=2
through
100
and (b) standard MCMC thinning across twelve experiments targeting challenging differential equation posterior distributions.
We conclude with a discussion of our results, related work, and future directions in
Section

8
and defer all proofs to the appendices.

Notation

We define the shorthand
[n]\triangleq\{1,\dots,n\}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
a\wedge b\triangleq\min(a,b)
for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
,
\real{}_{+}\triangleq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{x\in\real:x\geq 0}}\right\}
, and

\mathcal{B}(x;r)\triangleq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mid\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x-y}}}\right\|_{2}<r}}\right\}
for
r\in\mathbb{R}
. We use
\mathrm{Vol}(\mathcal{B})
to denote the volume of a compact set
\mathcal{B}\subset\real^{d}
.
We let
\mathcal{A}^{c}
denote the complement of a set
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{A}}(x)=1
if
x\in\mathcal{A}
and 0 otherwise.
We use
\mathrm{Pr}(\mathcal{E})
to denote the probability of an event
\mathcal{E}
.
For real-valued kernels
\mathbf{k}
and functions
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we make frequent use of the norms

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\mathbf{k}}}}\right\|_{\infty}=\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\mathbf{k}(x,y)|
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{f}}}\right\|_{\infty}=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(x)|
. For
x>0
, we use
\Gamma(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{x-1}e^{-t}\,dt
to denote the Gamma function (with
\Gamma(n)=(n-1)!
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
).

For two sequences of real numbers
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, we say that
a_{n}
is of order
b_{n}
and write
a_{n}=\mathcal{O}(b_{n})
or
a_{n}\precsim b_{n}
to denote that
a_{n}\leq cb_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and some constant
c>0
. We write
a_{n}=\Omega(b_{n})
if
b_{n}=\mathcal{O}(a_{n})
and
a_{n}=\Theta(b_{n})
when
a=\Omega(b_{n})
and
a=\mathcal{O}(b_{n})
. Moreover, we use
a_{n}=\mathcal{O}_{d}(b_{n}),a_{n}\precsim_{d}b_{n},a_{n}=\Omega_{d}(b_{n}),a_{n}\succsim_{d}b_{n}
to indicate dependency of underlying universal constant on
d
. We say
a_{n}=o(b_{n})
if
\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}/b_{n}=0
.
For a sequence of real-valued random variables
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, we write
X_{n}=\mathcal{O}_{P}(a_{n})
or
X_{n}=\mathcal{O}(a_{n})
in probability, when
\frac{X_{n}}{a_{n}}
is stochastically bounded, i.e., for all
\delta>0
, there exists finite
c_{\delta}
and
n_{\delta}
such that
\mathrm{Pr}(|\frac{X_{n}}{a_{n}}|>c_{\delta})<\delta
, for all
n>n_{\delta}
. We write
X_{n}=\Omega_{P}(a_{n})
if
1/X_{n}=\mathcal{O}_{P}(1/b_{n})
and
X_{n}=o_{p}(a_{n})
when
\frac{X_{n}}{a_{n}}\to 0
in probability, i.e., for all
\varepsilon>0
,
\mathrm{Pr}(|\frac{X_{n}}{a_{n}}|\geq\varepsilon)\to 0
.

We write
order
(n,\varepsilon)
-MMD (or
L^{\infty}
) coreset
to mean an
(n,\mathcal{O}(\epsilon))
-MMD (or
L^{\infty}
) coreset and append
in probability
to mean an
(n,\mathcal{O}_{p}(\epsilon))
-MMD (or
L^{\infty}
) coreset.

2
Input Point and Kernel Requirements

Given a target distribution
\mathbb{P}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, a kernel
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
satisfying
1
, and a sequence of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued input points
\mathcal{S}_{n}\!=\!(x_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
generated either randomly or deterministically, our goal is to identify a better-than-i.i.d. thinned MMD coreset, that is, a subsequence
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{out}}
of size
n^{\frac{1}{2}}
satisfying
\mmd_{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{out}})\!=\!o_{p}(n^{-\frac{1}{4}})
.
When drawing asymptotic conclusions, we will view
d
as fixed and
\mathcal{S}_{n}
as a prefix of an infinite sequence of points
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}\triangleq(x_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
.

2.1
Input point requirements

Our algorithms are designed to return high quality MMD coresets for the
input

\mathcal{S}_{n}
.
To translate these into high quality coresets for the target
\mathbb{P}
, it suffices, by the triangle inequality, for the input points to have quality
\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{S}_{n})=\mathcal{O}_{p}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})
.
As we discuss in
Section

8.1
, input sequences generated by i.i.d. sampling, kernel herding
(
Chen et al. 2010
)
, Stein Point MCMC
(
Chen et al. 2019
)
, and greedy sign selection
(
Karnin and Liberty 2019
)
all satisfy this property. Moreover, we prove in
Appendix

B
that an analogous guarantee holds for the iterates of a fast-mixing Markov chain.

Proposition 1

(MMD guarantee for MCMC)
.

Consider a homogeneous
\phi
-irreducible geometrically ergodic Markov chain

(
Gallegos-Herrada et al. 2023
, Thm. 1xi)
with initial state
x_{0}
, subsequent iterates
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
, and stationary distribution
\mathbb{P}
.
If
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
satisfies
1
, then
there exists a
\mathbb{P}
-almost everywhere finite function
c:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to(0,\infty]
such that, for any given
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta\in(0,1)
,

\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{S}_{n})\leq\sqrt{\frac{c(x_{0})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}}}\right\|_{\infty}\log(e/\delta)}{n}}

with probability
1-\delta
given
x_{0}
.

The
input radius
,

\textstyle R_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}\!\triangleq\!\max_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x}}}\right\|_{2},

(3)

will also play an important role in our results. In particular, the growth rate of this radius as a function of
n
impacts the growth rate of our MMD bounds. Our next definition assigns familiar names to the most commonly encountered growth rates.

Definition 4

(Input radius growth rates)
.

We say the point sequence
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
with prefixes
\mathcal{S}_{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
is

Compact
if
R_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}=\mathcal{O}_{d}(1)
,
SubGauss
if
R_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}=\mathcal{O}_{d}(\sqrt{\log n})
,
SubExp
if
R_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}=\mathcal{O}_{d}(\log n)
, and
\textsc{HeavyTail}(\rho)
with
\rho>0
if
R_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}=\mathcal{O}_{d}(n^{1/\rho})
.

These growth rates are exactly those which arise with probability
1
when an input sequence is generated identically from
\mathbb{P}
with corresponding tail behavior or from a fast-mixing Markov chain targeting
\mathbb{P}
.
Our proof of this result is given in
Appendix

C
.

Proposition 2

(Almost sure radius growth)
.

Consider either
(i) points
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
sampled identically (but not necessarily independently) from
\mathbb{P}
with
x_{0}
independent
or
(ii) a homogeneous
\phi
-irreducible geometrically ergodic Markov chain
with initial state
x_{0}
, subsequent iterates
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
, and stationary distribution
\mathbb{P}
.
Then the following statements hold true for any nonnegative
c
and
\rho
and
\mathbb{P}
-almost every
x_{0}
.

(a)

If
\mathbb{P}
is compactly supported, then, with probability
1
conditional on
x_{0}
,
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
is
Compact
.

(b)

If
\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathbb{P}}[e^{c\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{X}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}}]<\infty
, then, with probability
1
conditional on
x_{0}
,
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
is
SubGauss
.

(c)

If
\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathbb{P}}[e^{c\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{X}}}\right\|_{2}}]<\infty
, then, with probability
1
conditional on
x_{0}
,
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
is
SubExp
.

(d)

If
\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathbb{P}}[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{X}}}\right\|_{2}^{\rho}]<\infty
, then, with probability
1
conditional on
x_{0}
,
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
is
\textsc{HeavyTail}(\rho)
.

Finally, we will also require
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
to be
oblivious
, that is, generated independently of any randomness in the thinning algorithm. To capture this assumption, we treat
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
as fixed and deterministic hereafter. This treatment is without loss of generality since our results hold conditional on the observed values of
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
when the points are random and oblivious.

2.2
Kernel requirements

We use the terms
reproducing kernel
and
kernel
interchangeably to indicate that
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}

is symmetric and positive definite, i.e., that the kernel matrix
({\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(z_{i},z_{j}))_{i,j=1}^{l}
is symmetric and positive semidefinite for any evaluation points
(z_{i})_{i=1}^{l}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
In addition to
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
, our algorithm takes as input a
square-root kernel
for
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
.

Definition 5

(Square-root kernel)
.

We say a kernel
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is a
square-root
kernel
for
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
if
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}(x,\cdot)
is square integrable for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with

\textstyle{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(x,y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}(x,z){\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}(y,z)dz\quad\text{for all}\quad x,y\in\real^{d}.

(4)

We highlight that a square-root kernel need not be unique and that its existence is an indication of a certain degree of smoothness in the target kernel
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
. One convenient tool for deriving square-root kernels is the notion of a
spectral density
.

Definition 6

(Shift invariance and spectral density)
.

We call a kernel of the form
\mathbf{k}(x,y)=\kappa(x-y)
for
\kappa:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}

shift-invariant
and say
\mathbf{k}
has
spectral density

\widehat{\kappa}
if
\kappa
is the Fourier transform of
a finite measure with Lebesgue density
\widehat{\kappa}
, i.e.,
\kappa(z)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\int e^{-i\langle{\omega},{z}\rangle}\widehat{\kappa}(\omega)d\omega
.

{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(x,y)=\kappa(x\!-\!y)
Name of kernel

\kappa(z)
Expression for

\widehat{\kappa}(\omega)
Fourier transform

{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
Square-root kernel

\sigma>0
Gaussian
(\sigma):

\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{z}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}}\right)

\sigma^{d}\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{\sigma^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\omega}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}}{2}}}\right)

(

,

where

.

for our derivation.

As we show in
Appendices

N
and
Q
, many familiar kernels admit spectral densities, including Gaussian, Matérn, B-spline, inverse multiquadric, sech, and Wendland’s compactly supported kernels.
Moreover, by Bochner’s theorem
(
Bochner 1933
;
Wendland 2004
, Thm. 6.6)
and the Fourier inversion theorem
(
Wendland 2004
, Cor. 5.24)
, any continuous
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(x,y)=\kappa(x-y)
with absolutely integrable
\kappa
has a spectral density equal to the Fourier transform of
\kappa
.
Our next result (proved in
Appendix

D
) derives a square-root kernel for any shift-invariant
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
with a square-root integrable spectral density.

Proposition 3

(Shift-invariant square-root kernels)
.

If a kernel
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(x,y)\!=\!\kappa(x\!-\!y)
admits a spectral density (
Definition

6
)
\widehat{\kappa}

with
\int\sqrt{\widehat{\kappa}(\omega)}d\omega\!<\!\infty
, then
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}(x,y)\!=\!\frac{{\kappa}_{\mathrm{rt}}(x-y)}{(2\pi)^{d/4}}

is a square-root kernel of
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
for
{\kappa}_{\mathrm{rt}}
the
Fourier transform of
\sqrt{\widehat{\kappa}}
.

Table

1
gives several examples of common kernels satisfying the conditions of
Proposition

3
along with their associated square-root kernels. For example,
if
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
is Gaussian with bandwidth
\sigma
, then a rescaled Gaussian kernel with bandwidth
\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}
is a valid choice for
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
.
For simplicity, our results in the sequel assume the use of an exact square-root kernel
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
, but, as we detail in
Appendix

Q
, it suffices to use the square-root of any kernel that dominates
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
in the positive-definite order (see
Definition

8
).
For example, we show in
Proposition

4
of
Appendix

Q
that a standard Matérn kernel is a suitable square-root dominating kernel for any sufficiently-smooth shift-invariant
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
with absolutely integrable
\kappa
.
In
Table

5
of
Appendix

Q
, we also derive convenient tailored square-root dominating kernels for inverse multiquadric, sech, and Wendland’s compactly supported kernels.

Finally, we define several kernel growth and decay properties that will be explicitly assumed in some of our results.

Assumption 2

(Lipschitz kernel)
.

The kernel
\mathbf{k}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
admits a Lipschitz constant

\textstyle L_{\mathbf{k}}\triangleq\sup_{x,y,z}\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbf{k}(x,y)-\mathbf{k}(x,z)}}\right|}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{y-z}}}\right\|_{2}}<\infty.

(5)

Assumption 3

(Kernel tail decay)
.

The kernel
\mathbf{k}
satisfies
1
and, for each
\varepsilon>0
,

\textstyle\begin{split}\textstyle&\textstyle\max\{\ \inf\{r:\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y:\\
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x-y}}}\right\|_{2}\geq r\end{subarray}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathbf{k}(x,y)}}\right|\leq\varepsilon\},\quad\inf\{r:\tau_{\mathbf{k}}(r)\leq\varepsilon\}\ \}<\infty,\\
\textstyle&\textstyle\quad\text{where}\quad\tau_{\mathbf{k}}(r)\triangleq(\sup_{x}\int_{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{y}}}\right\|_{2}\geq r}\mathbf{k}^{2}(x,x-y)dy)^{\frac{1}{2}}\quad\text{for}\quad r\geq 0.\end{split}

The following definition gives
familiar names to commonly encountered tail decay rates.

Definition 7

(Kernel tail decay rate)
.

For a kernel
\mathbf{k}
satisfying
3
, define

\textstyle\begin{split}\textstyle R_{\mathbf{k},n}&\textstyle\triangleq\inf\{r:\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y:\\
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x-y}}}\right\|_{2}\geq r\end{subarray}}|\mathbf{k}(x,y)|\leq\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\mathbf{k}}}}\right\|_{\infty}}{n}\},\quad R_{\mathbf{k},n}^{\prime}\triangleq\inf\{r:\tau_{\mathbf{k}}(r)\leq\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\mathbf{k}}}}\right\|_{\infty}}{\sqrt{n}}\},\\
\textstyle\quad\text{and}\quad R_{\mathbf{k},n}^{\dagger}&\textstyle\triangleq\max\{R_{\mathbf{k},n},R_{\mathbf{k},n}^{\prime}\}\end{split}

for
\tau_{\mathbf{k}}
defined in
3
.
We say
\mathbf{k}
is (a)
Compact
if
R_{\mathbf{k},n}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{O}_{d}(1)
, (b)
SubGauss
if
R_{\mathbf{k},n}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{O}_{d}(\sqrt{\log n})
, (c)
SubExp
if
R_{\mathbf{k},n}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{O}_{d}(\log n)
, (d)
\textsc{HeavyTail}(\rho)
for
\rho>0
if
R_{\mathbf{k},n}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{O}_{d}(n^{1/\rho})
, and (e)
SubPoly
if
\log R_{\mathbf{k},n}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{O}_{d}(\log n)
.
Notably, any
Compact
,
SubGauss
,
SubExp
, or
\textsc{HeavyTail}(\rho)

\mathbf{k}
is also
SubPoly
.

Remark 1

(
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
tail decay implies
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
boundedness)
.

If
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
satisfying
3
is a square-root kernel of
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
,
then there exists a finite
r
for which
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(x,x)=\int{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}^{2}(x,x-y)dy\leq\|{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}\|_{\infty}^{2}\mathrm{Vol}(\mathcal{B}(0;r))+\tau_{{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}}^{2}(r)<\infty
.

Popular examples of
Compact
,
SubGauss
, and
SubExp

\mathbf{k}
are B-spline, Gaussian, and Matérn kernels respectively (see
Table

2
).
Moreover, one can directly verify that an inverse multiquadric
\mathbf{k}(x,y)=(\gamma^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x\!-\!y}}}\right\|_{2}^{2})^{-\nu}
with
\gamma>0
and
\nu>\frac{d}{4}
is
HeavyTail
​(
2\nu\wedge(4\nu\!-\!d)
).

Algorithm 2

kt-split
–

KT-SPLIT

The first stage,
kt-split
, is an initialization stage that partitions the input sequence
\mathcal{S}_{n}=(x_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
into
2^{m}
balanced candidate coresets, each of size
\lfloor{\frac{n}{2^{m}}}\rfloor
.
4
4

4

When
2^{m}
does not evenly divide
n
, the final
n-2^{m}\lfloor{\frac{n}{2^{m}}}\rfloor
points are discarded.

As depicted in
Fig.

1
, this partitioning is carried out recursively in
m
rounds, first dividing the input sequence in half, then halving those halves into quarters, and so on until coresets of size
\lfloor{\frac{n}{2^{m}}}\rfloor
are produced. The details of
kt-split
can appear a bit complicated as, in practice, all
m
halving rounds are carried out concurrently in an online manner.
However, under the hood, each candidate coreset is generated by recursively applying
a new simple subroutine called
kernel halving
.

Kernel halving

Kernel halving (KH,
Algorithm

3
) is a simple randomized procedure for dividing an input sequence
\mathcal{S}_{n}
into two balanced, equal-sized coresets
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{S}^{(2)}
using a kernel
\mathbf{k}
.
KH begins with two empty coresets
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{S}^{(2)}

and adds points
(x,x^{\prime})
from the input sequence two at a time, assigning one point from each pair to each coreset.
To encourage balance between the coresets during generation, KH effectively computes which assignment of
(x,x^{\prime})
leads to a smaller
\mmd_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathcal{S}^{(1)},\mathcal{S}^{(2)})
and then favors that assignment using non-uniform randomness.
More precisely, on the
i
-th step with
(x,x^{\prime})=(x_{2i-1},x_{2i})
, KH computes
the imbalance contrast

\alpha_{i}=i^{2}\big(\mmd_{\mathbf{k}}^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{(1)}\cup\{x\},\mathcal{S}^{(2)}\cup\{x^{\prime}\})-\mmd_{\mathbf{k}}^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{(1)}\cup\{x^{\prime}\},\mathcal{S}^{(2)}\cup\{x\})\big)

(6)

and then adds
x
to
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}
and
x^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{S}^{(2)}
or
x^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}
and
x
to
\mathcal{S}^{(2)}
with probability
biased toward the more balanced outcome.
We refer to this step as
probabilistic swapping
in the algorithm statements.
The exact value of this probability depends on a
swapping threshold

\mathfrak{a}_{i}
that is produced automatically each round based on the user-supplied inputs
(\delta_{i})_{i=1}^{\lfloor{n/2}\rfloor}
.
In
Section

4.1
, we will learn
how to set these inputs to achieve better balance than standard thinning or uniform subsampling, and in
Section

6.1
we will discuss the role of
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
and its generation procedure
get_swap_params
in achieving this balance.

Notably, in the context of
kt-split
, KH is run specifically with the square-root kernel
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
rather than the target kernel
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
.
This choice enables us to take advantage of the strong
L^{\infty}
balance properties established for KH in
Section

6
and the close connection between square-root
L^{\infty}
error and target MMD error revealed in
Section

4.2
.

Input:

kernel
\mathbf{k}
, point sequence
\mathcal{S}_{n}=(x_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
, probability sequence
(\delta_{i})_{i=1}^{\lfloor{n/2}\rfloor}

\mathcal{S}^{(1)},\mathcal{S}^{(2)}\leftarrow\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{}}\right\}
;
\psi_{0}\leftarrow{\boldsymbol{0}}\in\mathcal{H}
/​/ Initialize empty coresets:
\mathcal{S}^{(1)},\mathcal{S}^{(2)}
have size
i
after round
i

\sigma_{0}\leftarrow 0
/​/ Swapping parameter

for

i=1,2,\ldots,\lfloor{n/2}\rfloor

do

/​/ Construct kernel difference function using next two points

(x,x^{\prime})\leftarrow(x_{2i-1},x_{2i})
;
f_{i}\leftarrow\mathbf{k}(x_{2i-1},\cdot)-\mathbf{k}(x_{2i},\cdot)
;
\eta_{i}\leftarrow-1

/​/ Compute swapping threshold
\mathfrak{a}_{i}

\mathfrak{a}_{i},\sigma_{i}\leftarrow
get_swap_params(
\sigma_{i-1},\mathfrak{b},\delta_{i}
)

with
\mathfrak{b}^{2}\!=\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{f_{i}}}}\right\|_{\mathbf{k}}^{2}\!=\!\mathbf{k}(x,x)\!+\!\mathbf{k}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime})\!-\!2\mathbf{k}(x,x^{\prime})

/​/ Compute RKHS inner product
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\psi_{i-1},f_{i}}}\right\rangle_{\mathbf{k}}
, which has a simple form

\alpha_{i}\leftarrow\sum_{j=1}^{2i-2}(\mathbf{k}(x_{j},x)-\mathbf{k}(x_{j},x^{\prime}))-2\sum_{z\in\mathcal{S}^{(1)}}(\mathbf{k}(z,x)-\mathbf{k}(z,x^{\prime}))

/​/ Assign one point to each coreset after probabilistic swapping

(x,x^{\prime})\leftarrow(x^{\prime},x)

and

\eta_{i}\leftarrow 1

with probability

\min(1,\frac{1}{2}(1-\frac{\alpha_{i}}{\mathfrak{a}_{i}})_{+})

\mathcal{S}^{(1)}\texttt{.append}(x);\ \ \ \mathcal{S}^{(2)}\texttt{.append}(x^{\prime});\ \ \ \psi_{i}\leftarrow\psi_{i-1}+\eta_{i}f_{i}
/​/
\psi_{i}=\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{(2)}}\!\mathbf{k}(x^{\prime},\cdot)\!-\!\sum_{x\in\mathcal{S}^{(1)}}\!\mathbf{k}(x,\cdot)

end for

return

\mathcal{S}^{(1)}
, coreset of size

\lfloor{n/2}\rfloor

Algorithm 3

Kernel Halving

KT-SWAP

The second stage,
kt-swap
, refines the candidate coresets produced by
kt-split
in three steps. First,
kt-swap
adds a baseline coreset of size
\lfloor{\frac{n}{2^{m}}}\rfloor
to the candidate list (for example, one produced by standard thinning or uniform subsampling) to ensure that the
kt-swap
output is never worse than that of the baseline. Next, it selects the candidate coreset closest to
\mathcal{S}_{n}
in terms of
\mmd_{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
. Finally, it refines the selected coreset by replacing each coreset point in turn with the best alternative in
\mathcal{S}_{n}
, as measured by
\mmd_{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(\mathcal{S}_{n},\cdot)
.
This stage serves to greedily improve upon the MMD of the initial
kt-split
candidates, and, when computable,
\mmd_{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
to the target distribution
\mathbb{P}
can be substituted for the surrogate
\mmd_{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(\mathcal{S}_{n},\cdot)
throughout.

Input:

kernel
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
, point sequence
\mathcal{S}_{n}=(x_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
, candidate coresets
(\mathcal{S}^{(m,\ell)})_{\ell=1}^{2^{m}}

\mathcal{S}^{(m,0)}\!\leftarrow\!\texttt{baseline\_coreset}(\mathcal{S}_{n},\texttt{size}\!=\!\lfloor{n/2^{m}}\rfloor)
/​/ Compare to baseline (e.g., standard thinning)

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{KT}}\!\leftarrow\!\mathcal{S}^{(m,\ell^{\star})}\text{ for }\ell^{\star}\!\leftarrow\!\mathop{\mathrm{}}{argmin}_{\ell\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{0,1,\ldots,2^{m}}}\right\}}\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathcal{S}_{n},\mathcal{S}^{(m,\ell)})

/​/ Select best coreset

/​/ Swap out each point in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{KT}}
for best alternative in
\mathcal{S}_{n}

for

i=1,\ldots,\lfloor{n/2^{m}}\rfloor

do

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{KT}}[i]\leftarrow\mathop{\mathrm{}}{argmin}_{z\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathcal{S}_{n},\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{KT}}\text{ with }\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{KT}}[i]=z)

end for

return

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{KT}}
, refined coreset of size
\lfloor{n/2^{m}}\rfloor

Algorithm 4

kt-swap
–
Identify and refine the best candidate coreset

Complexity

For any
m
, the time complexity of kernel thinning is dominated by
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
kernel evaluations,
while the space complexity is
\mathcal{O}(n\min(d,n))
, achieved by storing the smaller of the input sequence
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
and the kernel matrix
({\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}(x_{i},x_{j}))_{i,j=1}^{n}
. In addition, scaling either
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
or
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
by a positive multiplier has no impact on
Algorithm

1
, so the kernels need only be specified up to arbitrary rescalings.

4
MMD Guarantees

We are now prepared to present our main MMD guarantees.

4.1
MMD guarantees for kernel thinning

Our first main result, proved in
Appendix

E
, bounds the MMD of a kernel thinning coreset in terms of the input
Eq.

3
and kernel
Definition

7
radii, the combined radii

\textstyle R_{\mathcal{S}_{n},\mathbf{k},n}\triangleq\min\big(R_{\mathcal{S}_{n}},n^{1+\frac{1}{d}}R_{\mathbf{k},n}+n^{\frac{1}{d}}\frac{\|\mathbf{k}\|_{\infty}}{L_{\mathbf{k}}}\big)\quad\text{and}\quad R_{\mathcal{S}_{n},\mathbf{k},n}^{\prime}\triangleq\max\big(R_{\mathcal{S}_{n}},R_{\mathbf{k},n}^{\prime}\big),

(7)

and the kernel thinning inflation factor

\textstyle\!\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{k}}(n,\!m,\!d,\!\delta,\!\delta^{\prime}\!,\!R)\!\triangleq\!2\,\mathbb{I}(\frac{n}{2^{m}}\!\not\in\!\mathbb{N})\!+\!37\sqrt{\!\log(\frac{6m}{2^{m}\delta})}\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\!\sqrt{\!\log({\frac{4}{\delta^{\prime}}})}\!+\!5\sqrt{{\!d\log\!\big(2\!+\!\!\frac{2L_{\mathbf{k}}}{\|\mathbf{k}\|_{\infty}}\!(R_{\mathbf{k},n}\!+\!R)\big)}}\!}}\right],

(8)

defined for any kernel
\mathbf{k}
satisfying
2
and
3
,
n,m,d\in\mathbb{N}
,
\delta\in(0,\frac{6m}{2^{m}}]
,
\delta^{\prime}\in(0,1]
, and
R\geq 0
.

Theorem 1

(MMD guarantee for kernel thinning)
.

Consider kernel thinning (
Algorithm

1
) with
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
satisfying
1
,

{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
a square-root kernel of
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
,

\delta^{\star}\triangleq\min_{i}\delta_{i}
, and

n_{\mathrm{out}}\triangleq\lfloor{{\textstyle\frac{n}{2^{m}}}}\rfloor
for
m\leq\lfloor{\log_{2}n}\rfloor
. If
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
satisfies
2
and
3
, then, for any fixed
\delta^{\prime}\!\in\!(0,1)
, we have

\textstyle\!\!\!\!\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathcal{S}_{n},\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{KT}})\!\leq\!\frac{\|{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}\|_{\infty}}{n_{\mathrm{out}}}\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[2\!+\!\sqrt{\frac{(4\pi)^{d/2}}{\Gamma(\frac{d}{2}\!+\!1)}}(R_{\mathcal{S}_{n},{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}},n_{\mathrm{out}}}^{\prime})^{\frac{d}{2}}\mathfrak{M}_{{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}}(n,m,\!d,\!\delta^{\star},\!\delta^{\prime},\!R_{\mathcal{S}_{n},{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}},n})}}\right],

(9)

with probability at least

1\!-\!\delta^{\prime}\!-\!\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{2^{j-1}}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{2^{m-j}n_{\mathrm{out}}}\delta_{i}
.

Remark 2

(Guarantee for target
\mathbb{P}
)
.

A guarantee for any target distribution
\mathbb{P}
follows directly from the triangle inequality,
\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{KT}})\leq\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{S}_{n})+\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathcal{S}_{n},\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{KT}})
.

Remark 3

(Comparison with baseline thinning)
.

The
kt-swap
step ensures that, deterministically,
\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathcal{S}_{n},\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{KT}})\leq\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathcal{S}_{n},\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{base}})

for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{base}}
a baseline thinned coreset of size
n_{\mathrm{out}}
. Therefore, we additionally have

\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{KT}})\leq 2\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{S}_{n})+\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{base}}).

Remark 4

(Finite-time and anytime guarantees)
.

To obtain a success probability of at least
1-\delta
with
\delta^{\prime}=\frac{\delta}{2}
, it suffices to choose
\delta_{i}=\frac{\delta}{n}
when the input size
n
is known in advance and
\delta_{i}=\frac{m\delta}{2^{m+2}(i+1)\log^{2}(i+1)}
when the input size
n
is not known in advance (but is chosen independently of the randomness used in kernel thinning). In either case,
\delta^{\star}\leq\frac{6m}{2^{m}}
is a valid argument to
\mathfrak{M}_{{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}}

Eq.

8
.
See
Appendix

F
for our proof.

Our next corollary, proved in
Appendix

G
, translates
Theorem

1

into specific rates of MMD decay depending on the radius growth of
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
and the tail decay of
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
.

Remark 5

(Probability parameters)
.

The condition
(\star)\ \sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{2^{j-1}}{m}\!\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor{n/2^{j}}\rfloor}\delta_{i}\!=\!o(1)
is satisfied when
\delta_{1}\!=\!\cdots\!=\!\delta_{\lfloor{\frac{n}{2}}\rfloor}\!=\!o(\frac{1}{n})
.
Hence, both
\log(1/\delta^{\star})\!=\!\mathcal{O}(\log n)
and
(\star)
are satisfied when, for example,
\delta_{1}\!=\!\cdots\!=\!\delta_{\lfloor{\frac{n}{2}}\rfloor}=\frac{1}{n\log\log n}
.

Corollary

1
shows that kernel thinning returns an
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},\mathcal{O}_{d}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{\log n}))
-MMD coreset in probability
when
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
and
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
are compactly supported.
For fixed
d
, this guarantee significantly improves upon the baseline
\Omega_{p}(n^{-\frac{1}{4}})
rates of i.i.d. sampling and standard MCMC thinning and matches the minimax lower bounds of
Section

8.1
up to a
\sqrt{\log n}
term and constants depending on
d
.
For example, when
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
is drawn i.i.d. from
\mathbb{P}
, kernel thinning is nearly minimax optimal amongst
all
distributional approximations (even weighted coresets and non-coreset approximations) that depend on
\mathbb{P}
only through
n
i.i.d. input points
(
Tolstikhin et al. 2017
, Thms. 1 and 6)
.

More generally, when

\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
and
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
are
SubGauss
,
SubExp
, or

\textsc{HeavyTail}(\rho)
with
\rho>2d
,

Corollary

1
shows that the kernel thinning provides an MMD error of
\mathcal{O}_{d}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(\log n)^{d/2+1}\log\log n})
,
\mathcal{O}_{d}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{(\log n)^{d+1}\log\log n}))
, and
\mathcal{O}_{d}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}n^{\frac{d}{2\rho}}\log n)
in probability with output coresets of size
\sqrt{n}
. In each case, we find that kernel thinning significantly improves upon an
\Omega_{p}(n^{-\frac{1}{4}})
baseline when
n
is sufficiently large relative to
d
and, by
Remark

3
, is never significantly worse than the baseline when
n
is small.
Our
SubExp
guarantees also resemble the classical quasi-Monte Carlo guarantees for the uniform distribution on
[0,1]^{d}
(see
Section

8.1
) but allow for non-uniform and unbounded target distributions
\mathbb{P}
.
