4.2
MMD coresets from square-root
L^{\infty}
coresets

Theorem

1
builds on a second key result, proved in
Appendix

H
, that bounds MMD error for
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
in terms of
L^{\infty}
error for the
square-root
kernel
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
.

Theorem 2

(MMD guarantee for square-root
L^{\infty}
approximations)
.

Suppose
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
satisfying
1
has a square-root kernel
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
satisfying
3
.
Then for any
distributions
\mu
and
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and scalars
r,a,b\geq 0
with

a+b=1
,

\displaystyle\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mu,\nu)\!\leq\!e_{d}r^{\frac{d}{2}}\!\cdot\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\mu{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}\!-\!\nu{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}}}}\right\|_{\infty}\!+2\tau_{{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}}(ar)\!+2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}}}\right\|_{\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}}\!\cdot\!\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\tau_{\mu}(br),\!\tau_{\nu}(br)}}\right\},

(10)

where
e_{d}\triangleq(\pi^{d/2}/\Gamma(d/2+1))^{\frac{1}{2}}
decreases super-exponentially in
d
and
\tau_{\mu}(r)\triangleq\mu(\mathcal{B}^{c}(0,r))
.

Importantly,
Theorem

2
implies that
any

L^{\infty}
coreset for the square-root kernel
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
, even one not produced by kernel thinning, is also an MMD coreset for the target kernel
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
with MMD error depending on the tail decay of
({\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}},\mu,\nu)
.

Corollary

2
summarizes the implications of
Theorem

2
for common classes of tail decay. See
Appendix

I
for the proof with explicit constants.

Corollary 2

(MMD error from square-root
L^{\infty}
error)
.

Under the setting and assumptions of
Theorem

2
,
define the
L^{\infty}
error
\varepsilon\triangleq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\mu{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}-\nu{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}}}}\right\|_{\infty}
and the tail decay function
\widetilde{\tau}(r)\!\triangleq\!\tau_{{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}}(r)\!+\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}}}\right\|_{\infty}^{1/2}\max\{\tau_{\mu}(r),\tau_{\nu}(r)\}
for
r\!\geq\!0
. Then the following implications hold for any nonnegative
c
and
\rho
.

Tail Decay

Compact

SubGauss

SubExp

\textsc{HeavyTail}(\rho)

\widetilde{\tau}(r)\precsim

\mathbb{I}(r\leq c)

e^{-cr^{2}}

e^{-cr}

r^{-\rho}

\Rightarrow\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mu,\nu)\precsim_{d}

\varepsilon

\varepsilon(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{\frac{d}{4}}

\varepsilon(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{\frac{d}{2}}

\varepsilon^{\frac{2\rho}{d+2\rho}}

Remark 6

(Tail decay from finite moments)
.

By Markov’s inequality
(
Durrett 2019
, Thm. 1.6.4)
,
\widetilde{\tau}
has (i)
Compact
decay when
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
is
Compact
and
\mu
and
\nu
have compact support; (ii)
SubGauss
decay when
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
is
SubGauss
and
\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mu}[e^{c\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{X}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}}],\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\nu}[e^{c\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{X}}}\right\|_{2}^{2}}]<\infty
; (iii)
SubExp
decay when
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
is
SubExp
and
\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mu}[e^{c\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{X}}}\right\|_{2}}],\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\nu}[e^{c\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{X}}}\right\|_{2}}]<\infty
; and (iv)
\textsc{HeavyTail}(\rho)
decay when
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
is
\textsc{HeavyTail}(\rho)
and
\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mu}[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{X}}}\right\|_{2}^{\rho}],\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\nu}[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{X}}}\right\|_{2}^{\rho}]<\infty
.

Corollary

2
highlights that MMD quality for
({\mathbf{k}_{\star}},\mu)
is of the same order as
L^{\infty}
quality for
({\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}},\mu)
when
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}},\mu,
and the approximation
\nu
have compact support. MMD quality then degrades naturally as the tail behavior worsens.
In
Sections

5
and
6
, we show that, with high probability,
kt-split
provides a high-quality
L^{\infty}
coreset for
({\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}},\mathcal{S}_{n})
and hence, by
Corollary

2
, also provides a high-quality MMD coreset for
({\mathbf{k}_{\star}},\mathcal{S}_{n})
.

5
Self-balancing Hilbert Walk

To exploit the
L^{\infty}
-MMD connection revealed in
Theorem

2
, we now turn our attention to constructing high-quality thinned
L^{\infty}
coresets.
Our strategy relies on a new Hilbert space generalization of the self-balancing walk of
Alweiss et al. 2021
.
We dedicate this section to defining and analyzing this
self-balancing Hilbert walk
, and we detail its connection to kernel thinning in
Section

6
.

Input:

sequence of functions
(f_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
in a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, threshold sequence
(\mathfrak{a}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}

\psi_{0}\leftarrow{\boldsymbol{0}}\in\mathcal{H}

for

i=1,2,\ldots,n

do

\alpha_{i}\leftarrow\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\psi_{i-1},f_{i}}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
/​/ Compute Hilbert space inner product

if

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\alpha_{i}}}\right|>\mathfrak{a}_{i}\textbf{:}

\psi_{i}\leftarrow\psi_{i-1}-f_{i}\cdot\alpha_{i}/\mathfrak{a}_{i}

else:

\eta_{i}\leftarrow 1\hskip 9.24994pt\text{with probability}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-{\alpha_{i}}/{\mathfrak{a}_{i}}}}\right)\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}\hskip 9.24994pt\eta_{i}\leftarrow-1\hskip 9.24994pt\text{otherwise}\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle\psi_{i}\leftarrow\psi_{i-1}+\eta_{i}f_{i}

end for

return

\psi_{n}
,
combination of signed input functions

Algorithm 5

Self-balancing Hilbert Walk

Alweiss et al. 2021
introduced
a randomized algorithm called the
self-balancing walk
that takes as
input a streaming sequence of Euclidean vectors
x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x_{i}}}}\right\|_{2}\leq 1
and outputs a online sequence of random assignments
\eta_{i}\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{-1,1}}\right\}
satisfying

\textstyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}x_{i}}}}\right\|_{\infty}\precsim\sqrt{\log(d/\delta)\log(n/\delta)}\quad\text{with probability at least $1-\delta$.}\quad

(11)

Since our ultimate aim is to combine kernel functions, we define a suitable Hilbert space generalization in
Algorithm

5
.

Given a streaming sequence of functions
f_{i}
in an arbitrary Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
with a norm
\|{\cdot}\|_{\mathcal{H}}
, this
self-balancing Hilbert walk
(SBHW) outputs a streaming sequence of signed function combinations
\psi_{i}
satisfying the following desirable properties established in
Appendix

J
.

Theorem 3

(Self-balancing Hilbert walk properties)
.

Consider the self-balancing Hilbert walk (
Algorithm

5
)
with each

f_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{i}>0

and define the
sub-Gaussian constants

\textstyle\sigma_{0}^{2}\triangleq 0\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma_{i}^{2}\triangleq\sigma_{i-1}^{2}+\|{f_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\Big(1+\frac{\sigma_{i-1}^{2}}{\mathfrak{a}_{i}^{2}}(\|{f_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}-2\mathfrak{a}_{i})\Big)_{+}\quad\forall i\geq 1.

(12)

The following properties hold true.

(i)

Functional sub-Gaussianity:

For each
i\in[n]
,

\psi_{i}
is
\sigma_{i}

sub-Gaussian
:

\textstyle\mathbb{E}[\exp(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\psi_{i},u}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}})]\leq\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{u}}}\right\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}}{2}}}\right)\quad\text{for all}\quad u\in\mathcal{H}.

(13)

(ii)

Signed sum representation:

If

\mathfrak{a}_{i}\geq\sigma_{i-1}\|{f_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\sqrt{2\log(2/\delta_{i})}

for
\delta_{i}\in(0,1]
, then, with probability at least
1-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{i}
,

\textstyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\alpha_{i}}}\right|\leq\mathfrak{a}_{i},\forall i\in[n],\quad\text{and}\quad\psi_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}f_{i}.

(14)

(iii)

Exact halving via symmetrization:

If

\mathfrak{a}_{i}\geq\sigma_{i-1}\|{f_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\sqrt{2\log(2/\delta_{i})}

for
\delta_{i}\in(0,1]
and each
f_{i}=g_{2i-1}-g_{2i}
for
g_{1},\dots,g_{2n}\in\mathcal{H}
, then, with prob. at least
1-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{i}
,

\textstyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\alpha_{i}}}\right|\leq\mathfrak{a}_{i},\forall i\in[n],\ \text{ and }\ \frac{1}{2n}\psi_{n}=\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{2n}g_{i}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}g_{i}\ \text{ for }\ \mathcal{I}=\{2i+\frac{\eta_{i}-1}{2}:i\in[n]\}.

(15)

(iv)

Pointwise sub-Gaussianity in RKHS:
If
\mathcal{H}
is the RKHS of a kernel
\mathbf{k}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
, then, for each
i\in[n]
and
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
\psi_{i}(x)
is
\sigma_{i}\sqrt{\mathbf{k}(x,x)}
sub-Gaussian:

\textstyle\mathbb{E}[\exp(\psi_{i}(x))]\leq\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}\mathbf{k}(x,x)}{2}}}\right).

(16)

(v)

Sub-Gaussian constant bound:

Fix any
q\in[0,1)
, and suppose
\frac{1}{2}\|{f_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\mathfrak{a}_{i}
for all
i\in[n]
.
If
\frac{\|{f_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}}{1+q}\leq\mathfrak{a}_{i}\leq\frac{\|{f_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}}{1-q}

whenever both

\sigma_{i-1}^{2}<\frac{\mathfrak{a}_{i}^{2}}{2\mathfrak{a}_{i}-\|{f_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}}

and

\|{f_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}>0
, then

\textstyle\sigma_{i}^{2}\leq\frac{\max_{j\in[i]}\|{f_{j}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}}{1-q^{2}}\quad\text{for all}\quad i\in[n].

(17)

(vi)

Adaptive thresholding:

If
\mathfrak{a}_{i}=\max(c_{i}\sigma_{i-1}\|{f_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}},\|{f_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2})
for
c_{i}\geq 0
,
then

\textstyle\sigma_{n}^{2}\leq\frac{\max_{i\in[n]}\|{f_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}}{4}(c^{\star}+1/c^{\star})^{2}\quad\text{for}\quad c^{\star}\triangleq\max_{i\in[n]}c_{i}.

(18)

Remark 7
.

The kernel
\mathbf{k}
in Property
(iv)
can be arbitrary and need not be bounded.

Property
(i)

ensures that the functions
\psi_{i}
produced by
Algorithm

5
are mean zero and unlikely to be large in any particular direction
u
.
Property
(ii)

builds on this functional sub-Gaussianity to ensure that
\psi_{n}
is precisely a sum of the signed input functions
\pm f_{i}
with high probability. The two properties together imply that, with high probability and an appropriate setting of
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
,
Algorithm

5
partitions the input functions
f_{i}
into two groups such that the function sums are nearly balanced across the two groups.
Property
(iii)

uses the signed sum representation to construct a two-thinned coreset for any input function sequence
(g_{i})_{i=1}^{2n}
. This is achieved by offering the consecutive function differences
g_{2i-1}-g_{2i}
as the inputs
f_{i}
to
Algorithm

5
.
Property
(iv)

highlights that functional sub-Gaussianity also implies sub-Gaussianity of the function values
\psi_{i}(x)
whenever the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
is an RKHS.
Finally, Properties
(v)
and
(vi)

provide explicit bounds on the sub-Gaussian constants
\sigma_{i}
when adaptive settings of the thresholds
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
are employed.
In
Section

6
, we will connect the SBHW to kernel halving and use Properties
Items

(iii)
,
(iv)
, and
(vi)
together to show that kernel halving and hence also
kt-split
coresets have provably small
L^{\infty}
kernel error.
Specifically, we will boost the pointwise sub-Gaussianity (Property
(iv)
) of the output function
\psi_{n}
into a high probability bound for
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\psi_{n}}}}\right\|_{\infty}=\sup_{x}|\psi_{n}(x)|=\sup_{x}|\langle{\psi_{n}},{\mathbf{k}(x,\cdot)}\rangle|
by constructing a finite cover for
(\psi_{n}(x))_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
based on the decay and smoothness of the kernel
\mathbf{k}
.

Comparison with i.i.d. signs

A simple alternative to
Algorithm

5
is to assign signs uniformly at random to each vector, that is, to output
\psi_{n}^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}^{\prime}f_{i}
with independent Rademacher
\eta_{i}^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}
.
Since the minimal squared sub-Gaussian constant of a sum of independent weighted Rademachers is equal to its variance
(
Buldygin and Kozachenko 1980
, Lem. 5 & Ex. 1)
,
the minimal squared sub-Gaussian constant of
\psi_{n}^{\prime}
satisfies

\textstyle\sigma_{\mathrm{IID}}^{2}=\sup_{u\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{Var}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\psi_{n}^{\prime},u}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}})/\|{u}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\sup_{u\in\mathcal{H}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle f_{i},u}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}/\|{u}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}.

(19)

In the best case, all
f_{i}
are orthogonal and bounded and
\sigma_{\mathrm{IID}}^{2}=\max_{i\in[n]}\|{f_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
does not grow with
n
; the reader can check that
Algorithm

5
also reduces to i.i.d. signing in this case.
However, in the worst case, all
f_{i}
are equal, and
\sigma_{\mathrm{IID}}^{2}=n\max_{i\in[n]}\|{f_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
.
In contrast,
if we choose
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
as in Property
Item

(vi)
with
c_{i}=\sqrt{2\log(2n/\delta)}
, then the SBHW output

\psi_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}f_{i}
with probability
1-\delta
by Property
Item

(ii)
and has squared sub-Gaussian constant
\sigma_{\mathrm{SBHW}}^{2}=\mathcal{O}(\log(2n/\delta)\max_{i\in[n]}\|{f_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2})
in every case by Property
Item

(vi)
.
This drop from
\Omega(n)
to
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
represents an exponential improvement in worst-case balance over employing i.i.d. signs.

We can attribute this gain to the carefully chosen updates of
Algorithm

5
.
Notice that, on round
i
, the function
\psi_{i-1}
is updated only in the
f_{i}
direction, so it suffices to examine the evolution of
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\psi_{i-1},f_{i}}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
.
We show in
Section

J.1
that this evolution takes the form

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\psi_{i},f_{i}}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=(1-\|{f_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}/\mathfrak{a}_{i})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\psi_{i-1},f_{i}}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}+\varepsilon_{i}\|{f_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

(20)

where
\varepsilon_{i}\triangleq\mathbb{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\alpha_{i}}}\right|\leq\mathfrak{a}_{i}}}}\right](\eta_{i}+\alpha_{i}/\mathfrak{a}_{i})
is mean-zero and
1
-sub-Gaussian given
\psi_{i-1}
.
In other words, whenever
\mathfrak{a}_{i}\geq\|{f_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
(as recommended in Property
(vi)
),
Algorithm

5
first shrinks the magnitude of
\psi_{i-1}
in the
f_{i}
direction before adding a sub-Gaussian variable in this direction.
This targeted shrinkage is absent in the i.i.d. signing update,

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\psi_{i}^{\prime},f_{i}}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\psi_{i-1}^{\prime},f_{i}}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}+\eta_{i}^{\prime}\|{f_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2},

(21)

which simply adds a sub-Gaussian variable in the
f_{i}
direction, and allows the SBHW to maintain a substantially smaller sub-Gaussian constant.

General recipe for exact halving

The symmetrization construction introduced in Property
(iii)
can be used to convert any vector balancing algorithm (i.e., any algorithm which assigns
\pm 1
signs to a sequence of vectors) into an exact halving algorithm (i.e., one which assigns
-1
to exactly half of the points) simply by running the algorithm on paired vector differences.
We will use this property in the sequel to painlessly construct coresets of an exact target size.

Comparison with the self-balancing walk of
Alweiss et al. 2021

In the Euclidean setting with
\mathcal{H}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, constant thresholds
\mathfrak{a}_{i}=30\log(n/\delta)
, and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\psi_{i-1},f_{i}}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
the usual Euclidean dot product,
Algorithm

5
recovers a slight variant of the Euclidean self-balancing walk of
Alweiss et al. 2021
.
The original algorithm differs only superficially by terminating with failure whenever
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\alpha_{i}}}\right|>\mathfrak{a}_{i}
.
We allow the walk to continue with the update
\psi_{i}\leftarrow\psi_{i-1}-f_{i}\cdot\alpha_{i}/\mathfrak{a}_{i}
, as it streamlines our sub-Gaussianity analysis and avoids the reliance on distributional symmetry present in Sec. 2.1 of
Alweiss et al. 2021
.
We show in
Appendix

R
that
Theorem

3
recovers the guarantee
Eq.

11
of
Alweiss et al. 2021
with improved constants and a less conservative setting of
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
.

6
L^{\infty}
Guarantees

In this section, we derive near-optimal
L^{\infty}
coreset guarantees for kernel halving (
Algorithm

3
) and
kt-split
by relating the two algorithms to the self-balancing Hilbert walk (
Algorithm

5
).

6.1
L^{\infty}
guarantees for kernel halving

To make the connection between KH and SBHW more apparent, we have translated each line of

Algorithm

3
into the notation of
Algorithm

5
.
In this notation, we see that
Algorithm

3
forms signed combinations
\psi_{i}
of paired kernel differences
f_{i}=\mathbf{k}(x_{2i-1},\cdot)-\mathbf{k}(x_{2i},\cdot)
; that
the inner product
\alpha_{i}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\psi_{i-1},f_{i}}}\right\rangle_{\mathbf{k}}
has a simple explicit form in terms of kernel evaluations; and
that, under the event
\mathcal{E}=\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\alpha_{i}}}\right|\leq\mathfrak{a}_{i}\ \text{for all}\ i=1,\ldots,\lfloor{n/2}\rfloor\}
, the function
\psi_{\lfloor{n/2}\rfloor}
of
Algorithm

3
exactly matches the output of SBHW.
Indeed, the function
get_swap_params
serves to compute the sub-Gaussian constants
\sigma_{i}
exactly as defined in
Theorem

3
and to adaptively select the thresholds
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
exactly as recommended in
Theorem

3
Items

(iii)
and
(vi)
:
\mathfrak{a}_{i}=\max(\|{f_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\sigma_{i-1}\sqrt{2\log(2/\delta_{i})},\|{f_{i}}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2})
.

This choice of
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
simultaneously ensures that the KH-SBHW equivalence event
\mathcal{E}
occurs with high probability by
Theorem

3
Item

(iii)
and that the SBHW sub-Gaussian constant remains small by
Theorem

3
Item

(vi)
.
Hence, we can
invoke the pointwise SBHW sub-Gaussianity revealed in
Theorem

3
Item

(iv)

to control the KH
L^{\infty}
coreset error with high probability.

Theorem 4

(
L^{\infty}
guarantees for kernel halving)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{KH}}(\mathbf{k},\mathcal{S},\Delta)
denote the output of kernel halving (
Algorithm

3
) with kernel
\mathbf{k}
satisfying
2
and
3
, input point sequence
\mathcal{S}
, and probability sequence
\Delta
.
Let
\mathbb{P}_{n}\triangleq{\textstyle\frac{1}{n}}{\textstyle\sum}_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{x_{i}}
, and
recall the definitions of
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{k}}

Eq.

8

and
R_{\mathcal{S}_{n},\mathbf{k},n}

Eq.

7
.
The following statements hold for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m\leq\log_{2}n
and
\delta^{\prime}\in(0,1)
.

(a)

Kernel halving yields a
2
-thinned
L^{\infty}
coreset:
The output
\mathcal{S}^{(1)}\triangleq\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{KH}}(\mathbf{k},\mathcal{S}_{n},(\delta_{i})_{i=1}^{\lfloor{\frac{n}{2}}\rfloor})
has size
n_{\mathrm{out}}=\lfloor{\frac{n}{2}}\rfloor
with
{\mathbb{Q}}_{\mathrm{KH}}^{(1)}\!\triangleq\!{\textstyle\frac{1}{{n_{\mathrm{out}}}}}{\textstyle\sum}_{x\in\mathcal{S}^{(1)}}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{x}

satisfying

\textstyle\|\mathbb{P}_{n}\mathbf{k}-{\mathbb{Q}}_{\mathrm{KH}}^{(1)}\mathbf{k}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\|\mathbf{k}\|_{\infty}}{n_{\mathrm{out}}}\cdot\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{k}}(n,1,d,\delta^{\star},\delta^{\prime},R_{\mathcal{S}_{n},\mathbf{k},n})

(22)

with probability at least
1\!-\!\delta^{\prime}\!-\!\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\mathrm{out}}}\delta_{i}

for
\delta^{\star}\triangleq\min_{i\leq n_{\mathrm{out}}}\delta_{i}
.

(b)

Repeated kernel halving yields a
2^{m}
-thinned
L^{\infty}
coreset:

For each
j>1
, let
\mathcal{S}^{(j)}\triangleq\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{KH}}(\mathbf{k},\mathcal{S}^{(j-1)},(\frac{2^{j-1}}{m}\delta_{i})_{i=1}^{\lfloor{n/2^{j}}\rfloor})

be the output of kernel halving recursively applied for
j
rounds. Then

\mathcal{S}^{(m)}
has size
n_{\mathrm{out}}=\lfloor{\frac{n}{2^{m}}}\rfloor
with
{\mathbb{Q}}_{\mathrm{KH}}^{(m)}\!\triangleq\!{\textstyle\frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{out}}}}{\textstyle\sum}_{x\in\mathcal{S}^{(m)}}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{x}
satisfying

\textstyle\|\mathbb{P}_{n}\mathbf{k}\!-\!\mathbb{Q}^{(m)}_{\mathrm{KH}}\mathbf{k}\|_{\infty}

\textstyle\leq\frac{\|\mathbf{k}\|_{\infty}}{n_{\mathrm{out}}}\cdot\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{k}}(n,m,d,\delta^{\star},\delta^{\prime},R_{\mathcal{S}_{n},\mathbf{k},n})

(23)

with probability at least
1\!-\!\delta^{\prime}\!-\!\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{2^{j-1}}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{2^{m-j}n_{\mathrm{out}}}\delta_{i}
for
\delta^{\star}\triangleq\min_{i\leq 2^{m}n_{\mathrm{out}}}\delta_{i}
.

Theorem

4
, proved in
Appendix

K
, shows that
L^{\infty}
error for KH scales simply as the kernel thinning inflation factor
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{k}}

Eq.

8
divided by the size of the output.
Our next corollary, an immediate consequence of

Theorem

4
Item

(b)
and the definition of
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{k}}

Eq.

8
, translates these bounds into rates of decay depending on the radius growth of
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
and the tail decay of
\mathbf{k}
.

Corollary 3

(
L^{\infty}
rates for kernel halving)
.

Under the notation and assumptions of
Theorem

4
,
consider a sequence of repeated kernel halving runs (
Algorithm

3
), indexed by
n\in\mathbb{N}
, with
m=\lfloor{\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}n}\rfloor
rounds,

\log(1/\delta^{\star})=\mathcal{O}(\log n)
, and
\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{2^{j-1}}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor{n/2^{j}}\rfloor}\delta_{i}=o(1)
as
n\to\infty
.
If
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
and
\mathbf{k}
respectively satisfy one of the radius growth (
Definition

4
) and tail decay (
Definition

7
) conditions in the table below,
then
\|\mathbb{P}_{n}\mathbf{k}\!-\!\mathbb{Q}^{(m)}_{\mathrm{KH}}\mathbf{k}\|_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{\infty,n})
in probability where
\varepsilon_{\infty,n}
is the corresponding table entry and all hidden constants are independent of the dimension
d
.

Type of
\mathbf{k}

Type of
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}

Compact
with
\frac{L_{\mathbf{k}}(R_{\mathbf{k},n}\wedge R_{\mathcal{S}_{n}})}{\|\mathbf{k}\|_{\infty}}\!=\!\mathcal{O}(1)
Compact
with
R_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}\!=\!\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{d})

SubPoly
with
\log(\frac{L_{\mathbf{k}}R_{\mathbf{k},n}}{\|\mathbf{k}\|_{\infty}})\!=\!\mathcal{O}(\log n)
Arbitrary

\boldsymbol{\varepsilon_{\infty,n}}

\sqrt{\frac{d\log n}{n}}

\sqrt{\frac{d}{n}}
​
\log n

Remark 8

(Example settings for KH
L^{\infty}
rates)
.

Remark

5
specifies settings of
(\delta_{i})_{i=1}^{\lfloor{n/2}\rfloor}
that meet the conditions of
Corollary

3
.
For any radial kernel
\mathbf{k}(x,y)=\kappa(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x-y}}}\right\|_{2}/\sigma)
with
L
-Lipschitz
\kappa:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, bandwidth
\sigma>0
, and
\|\mathbf{k}\|_{\infty}=1
, we have
\frac{L_{\mathbf{k}}}{\|\mathbf{k}\|_{\infty}}\leq\frac{L}{\sigma}
,
R_{\mathbf{k},n}\leq\sigma\kappa^{\dagger}({1}{/n})
, and therefore
\frac{L_{\mathbf{k}}R_{\mathbf{k},n}}{\|\mathbf{k}\|_{\infty}}\leq L\,\kappa^{\dagger}({1}{/n})
where
L
and
\kappa^{\dagger}(u)\triangleq\sup\{r:\kappa(r)\geq u\}
are independent of
d
and
\sigma
.
Hence Gaussian, Matérn (with
\nu>\frac{d}{2}+1
),
and IMQ kernels satisfy the
SubPoly
requirements of
Corollary

3
with any choice of bandwidth and satisfy the
Compact
requirements when restricted to a compact domain with
\sigma=\sqrt{d}
. See
Appendix

L
for our proof.

Near-optimal
L^{\infty}
coresets

For any bounded, radial
\mathbf{k}
satisfying mild decay and smoothness conditions,
Phillips and Tai 2020
proved that any procedure outputting a coreset of size
n^{\frac{1}{2}}
must suffer
\Omega(\min(\sqrt{d}n^{-\frac{1}{2}},n^{-\frac{1}{4}}))

L^{\infty}
error with constant probability for some
\mathbb{P}_{n}
. Hence, the repeated KH quality guarantees from
Corollary

3
are within a
\log n
factor of minimax optimality for this kernel family which includes Gaussian, Matérn, Wendland, and IMQ kernels.

Online vector balancing in an RKHS

In the online vector balancing problem of
Spencer 1977
, one must assign signs
\eta_{i}
to Euclidean vectors
f_{i}
in an online fashion while keeping the norm of the signed sum
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}f_{i}}}}\right\|_{\infty}
as small as possible.
As an immediate consequence of
Theorem

4
Item

(a)
, we find that kernel halving solves an RKHS generalization of the online vector balancing problem.

Corollary 4

(Online vector balancing in an RKHS)
.

Consider a sequence of kernel halving runs (
Algorithm

3
), indexed by
n\in\mathbb{N}
, with

\log(1/\delta^{\star})=\mathcal{O}(\log n)
and
\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor{n/2^{j}}\rfloor}\delta_{i}=o(1)
as
n\to\infty
. If the kernel
\mathbf{k}
is
SubPoly
(
Definition

7
) with
\log(\frac{L_{\mathbf{k}}R_{\mathbf{k},n}}{\|\mathbf{k}\|_{\infty}})=\mathcal{O}(\log n)
,
then
\|{\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\epsilon_{i}\mathbf{k}(x_{i},\cdot)}\|_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{d}\log n)
in probability for the generated signs
\epsilon_{i}\triangleq\eta_{\lceil{i/2}\rceil}(-1)^{i}
.

Proof

By
Theorem

4
Item

(a)
and the definition of
\mathfrak{M}_{\mathbf{k}}

Eq.

8
,
\|{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}f_{i}}\|_{\infty}\!=\!\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{d}\log n)
in probability. Noting that
-\eta_{i}f_{i}\!=\!\eta_{i}(\mathbf{k}(x_{2i},\cdot)\!-\!\mathbf{k}(x_{2i-1},\cdot))\!=\!\epsilon_{2i-1}\mathbf{k}(x_{2i-1},\cdot)+\epsilon_{2i}\mathbf{k}(x_{2i},\cdot)
, the claim follows.

Corollary

4
applies to
any
fixed input point sequence
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
and to a broad range of kernels including, by
Remark

8
, Gaussian, Matérn, and IMQ kernels, as well as B-spline kernel since
Eqs.

342
and
343
imply that
\log(\frac{L_{\mathbf{k}}R_{\mathbf{k},n}}{\|\mathbf{k}\|_{\infty}})=\mathcal{O}(\log d)=\mathcal{O}(\log n)
for B-spline
\mathbf{k}
.

6.2
L^{\infty}
and MMD guarantees for KT-SPLIT

We finally extend our
L^{\infty}
and MMD guarantees to the
kt-split
step of kernel thinning by observing that each candidate coreset generated by
kt-split
is the product of repeated kernel halving (
Algorithm

3
) with
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
as the chosen kernel
\mathbf{k}
.
Hence,
Theorem

4
Item

(a)
applies equally to the coreset
\mathcal{S}^{(1,1)}
returned by
kt-split
with
m=1
, and, when
m>1
,
Theorem

4
Item

(b)
applies to the coreset
\mathcal{S}^{(m,1)}
produced by
kt-split
.
Combining these
L^{\infty}
bounds with our
L^{\infty}
to MMD conversion theorem (
Theorem

2
) yields the following corollary proved in
Appendix

M
.

Corollary 5

(
L^{\infty}
and MMD guarantees for
kt-split
)
.

Consider
kt-split
with
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
satisfying
3
and
2
,

\delta^{\star}\triangleq\min_{i}\delta_{i}
,
and
\mathbb{P}_{n}\triangleq{\textstyle\frac{1}{n}}{\textstyle\sum}_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{x_{i}}
.
The guarantees of
Theorem

4
with
\mathbf{k}={\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
hold if
\mathbb{Q}^{(m,1)}_{\mathrm{KT}}\!\triangleq\!{\textstyle\frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{out}}}}{\textstyle\sum}_{x\in\mathcal{S}^{(m,1)}}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{x}
is substituted for
\mathbb{Q}^{(m)}_{\mathrm{KH}}
, and the guarantees of

Theorem

1

hold if the output coreset
\mathcal{S}^{(m,1)}
is substituted for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{KT}}
,

Corollary

5
ensures that, with high probability, at least one
kt-split
candidate is a high-quality
L^{\infty}
coreset for
({\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}},\mathbb{P}_{n})
and hence also a high-quality MMD coreset for
({\mathbf{k}_{\star}},\mathbb{P}_{n})
.
The proof of
Theorem

1
in
Appendix

E
establishes the same MMD guarantee for kernel thinning by noting that the subsequent
kt-swap
step directly minimizes the
\mmd_{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
to
\mathbb{P}_{n}
and hence can only improve or maintain this MMD quality.
Finally, exactly as in the proofs of
Corollaries

3
and
1
we can deduce both
L^{\infty}
and MMD rate bounds for
kt-split
coresets.

Corollary 6

(
L^{\infty}
and MMD rates for
kt-split
)
.

Under the notation and assumptions of
Corollary

5
, consider a sequence of
kt-split
runs, indexed by
n\in\mathbb{N}
, with
m=\lfloor{\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}n}\rfloor
rounds,

\log(1/\delta^{\star})=\mathcal{O}(\log n)
, and
\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{2^{j-1}}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor{n/2^{j}}\rfloor}\delta_{i}=o(1)
as
n\to\infty
.
The guarantees of
Corollary

3
with
\mathbf{k}={\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
hold if
\mathbb{Q}^{(m,1)}_{\mathrm{KT}}
is substituted for
\mathbb{Q}^{(m)}_{\mathrm{KH}}
, and the guarantees of
Corollary

1
hold if
\mathcal{S}^{(m,1)}
is substituted for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{KT}}
.

7
Vignettes

We complement our primary methodological and theoretical development with
two vignettes
illustrating
the promise of kernel thinning for improving upon (a) i.i.d. sampling in dimensions
d=2
through
100
and (b) standard MCMC thinning when targeting challenging differential equation posteriors.
See
Appendix

P
for
supplementary details and

https://github.com/microsoft/goodpoints

for a Python implementation of kernel thinning and
code replicating each vignette.

7.1
Common settings

Throughout, we adopt a
\textbf{Gaussian}(\sigma)
target kernel
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(x,y)=\exp(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x-y}}}\right\|_{2}^{2})

and the corresponding square-root kernel
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
from
Table

1
. To output a coreset of size
n^{\frac{1}{2}}
with
n
input points, we (a) take every
n^{\frac{1}{2}}
-th point for standard thinning and (b) run kernel thinning (KT) with
m=\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}n
using a standard thinning coreset as the base coreset in
kt-swap
. For each input sample size
n\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{2^{4},2^{6},\ldots,2^{14},2^{16}}}\right\}
with
\delta_{i}=\frac{1}{2n}
, we report the mean coreset error
\mmd_{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{S})

\pm 1
standard error across
10
independent replications of the experiment (the standard errors are too small to be visible in all experiments).
We additionally regress the log mean MMD onto the log input size using ordinary least squares and display both the best linear fit and an empirical decay rate based on the slope of that fit, e.g., for a slope of
-0.25
, we report an empirical decay rate of
n^{-0.25}
for the mean MMD.

Figure 2:
Kernel thinning (KT) and i.i.d. coresets for 4- and 8-component mixture of Gaussian targets with equidensity contours of the target underlaid.
See
Section

7.2
for more details.

7.2
Kernel thinning versus i.i.d. sampling

We first illustrate the benefits of kernel thinning over i.i.d. sampling from a target
\mathbb{P}
.
We generate each input sequence
\mathcal{S}_{n}
i.i.d. from
\mathbb{P}
, use squared kernel bandwidth
\sigma^{2}=2d
, and consider both Gaussian targets
\mathbb{P}=\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{d})
for
d\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{2,4,10,100}}\right\}
and
mixture of Gaussians (MoG) targets
\mathbb{P}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{j},\mathbf{I}_{2})
with
M\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{4,6,8}}\right\}
component locations
\mu_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
defined in
Section

P.1
.

Fig.

2
highlights the visible differences between the KT and i.i.d. coresets for the MoG targets.
Even for small sample sizes, the KT coresets achieves better stratification across components with less clumping and fewer gaps within components, suggestive of a better approximation to
\mathbb{P}
.
Indeed, when we examine MMD error as a function of coreset size in

Fig.

3
, we observe that kernel thinning provides a significant improvement across all settings.
For the Gaussian
d=2
target and each MoG target, the KT MMD error scales as
n^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, a quadratic improvement over the
\Omega_{p}(n^{-\frac{1}{4}})
MMD error of i.i.d. sampling.
Notably, we would not expect to see an exact empirical rate of
n^{-\frac{1}{2}}
for larger
d
and small
n
due to the logarithmic factors in our MMD bounds.
However, even for small sample sizes and high dimensions, we observe in
Fig.

3(b)
that KT significantly improves both the magnitude and the decay rate of MMD relative to i.i.d. sampling.

(a)

Mixture of Gaussians
\mathbb{P}

(b)

Gaussian
\mathbb{P}

Figure 3:
Kernel thinning versus i.i.d. sampling.

For (a) mixture of Gaussian targets with
M\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{4,6,8}}\right\}
components and (b) standard multivariate Gaussian targets in dimension
d\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{2,4,10,100}}\right\}
, kernel thinning (KT) reduces
\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{S})
significantly more quickly than the standard
n^{-\frac{1}{4}}
rate for
n^{\frac{1}{2}}
i.i.d. points, even in high dimensions. For reference, decay rates of
\sqrt{\log n/n}
and
\log n/\sqrt{n}
are plotted in each of the figures in panel (b).

7.3
Kernel thinning versus standard MCMC thinning

Next, we illustrate the benefits of kernel thinning over standard Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) thinning on twelve posterior inference experiments conducted by
Riabiz et al. 2021
.
We briefly describe each experiment here and refer the reader to
Riabiz et al. 2021
for more details.

Goodwin and Lotka-Volterra experiments

From
Riabiz et al. 2020
, we obtain the output of four distinct MCMC procedures targeting each of two
d=4
-dimensional posterior distributions
\mathbb{P}
: (1) a posterior over the parameters of the
Goodwin model
of oscillatory enzymatic control
(
Goodwin 1965
)
and (2) a posterior over the parameters of the
Lotka-Volterra model
of oscillatory predator-prey evolution
(
Lotka 1925
;
Volterra 1926
)
.
For each target,
Riabiz et al. 2020
provide
2\times 10^{6}
sample points from each of four MCMC algorithms: Gaussian random walk (RW), adaptive Gaussian random walk
(
Haario et al. 1999
, adaRW,)
, Metropolis-adjusted Langevin algorithm
(
Roberts and Tweedie 1996
, MALA,)
, and pre-conditioned MALA
(
Girolami and Calderhead 2011
, pMALA,)
.

Hinch experiments

From
Riabiz et al. 2020
, we also obtain the output of two independent Gaussian random walk MCMC chains for each of two
d=38
-dimensional posterior distributions
\mathbb{P}
: (1) a posterior over the parameters of the
Hinch model
of calcium signalling in cardiac cells
(
Hinch et al. 2004
)
and (2) a tempered version of the same posterior, as defined by
Riabiz et al. 2021
.
In computational cardiology, the calcium signalling model represents one component of a heart simulator, and one aims to propagate uncertainty in the signalling model through the whole heart simulation, an operation which requires thousands of CPU hours per sample point
(
Riabiz et al. 2021
)
.
In this setting, the costs of running kernel thinning are dwarfed by the time required to generate the input sample (two weeks) and more than offset by the cost savings in the downstream uncertainty propagation task.

Preprocessing and kernel settings

We discard the initial points of each chain as burn-in using the maximum burn-in period reported in
Riabiz et al. 2021
.
and normalize each Hinch chain by subtracting the post-burn-in sample mean and dividing each coordinate by its post-burn-in sample standard deviation.
To form an input sequence
\mathcal{S}_{n}
of length
n
for coreset construction, we downsample the remaining points using standard thinning.
Since exact computation of
\mmd_{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{S})
is intractable for these posterior targets, we report
\mmd_{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(\mathcal{S}_{n},\mathcal{S})
—the error that is controlled directly in our theoretical results—for these experiments.
We select the kernel bandwidth
\sigma
using the popular median heuristic
(
Garreau et al. 2017
, see, e.g.,)
. Additional details can be found in
Section

P.3
.

Figure 4:
Kernel thinning versus standard MCMC thinning.

Kernel thinning (KT) significantly improves both the rate of decay and the order of magnitude of mean
\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathcal{S}_{n},\mathcal{S})
in each posterior inference task, including eight tasks with 4-dimensional targets (Goodwin and Lotka-Volterra) and four tasks with 38-dimensional targets (Hinch).
See
Section

7.3
for more details.

Results

Fig.

4
compares the mean
\mmd_{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(\mathcal{S}_{n},\mathcal{S})
error of the generated kernel thinning and standard thinning coresets.
In each of the twelve experiments, KT significantly improves both the rate of decay and the order of magnitude of mean MMD, in line with the guarantees of
Theorem

1
.
Notably, in the
d=38
-dimensional Hinch experiments, standard thinning already improves upon the
n^{-\frac{1}{4}}
rate of i.i.d. subsampling but is outpaced by KT which consistently provides further improvements.

8
Discussion

We introduced kernel thinning (
Algorithm

1
), a new, practical solution to the thinned MMD coreset problem that, given
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
time and
\mathcal{O}(n\min(d,n))
storage, improves upon the integration error of i.i.d. sampling and standard MCMC thinning.
To achieve this we first showed that any
L^{\infty}
coreset for a square-root kernel
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
also provides an MMD coreset for its associated target kernel
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
(
Theorem

2
).
We next introduced and analyzed a self-balancing Hilbert walk for solving the online vector balancing problem in Hilbert spaces (
Algorithms

5
and
3
).
We then designed a symmetrized version of SBHW for RKHSes—kernel halving—that delivers
2
-thinned coresets with small
L^{\infty}
error (
Algorithms

3
and
4
).
Our online algorithm,
kt-split
, recursively applies kernel halving to a square-root kernel to obtain near-optimal
L^{\infty}
coresets in
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
time with
\mathcal{O}(n\min(d,n))
space (
Corollaries

3
and
5
).
Kernel thinning then combines
kt-split
with a greedy refinement step (
kt-swap
) to yield coresets with better-than-i.i.d. MMD for a broad range of kernels and target distributions (
Theorems

1
and
1
).
While our analysis is restricted to kernels that admit square-root dominating kernels,
Dwivedi and Mackey 2022
recently generalized the KT algorithm and analysis to support arbitrary kernels.
Separately,
Shetty et al. 2022
have developed a distribution compression meta-algorithm, Compress++, which reduces the runtime of KT to near-linear
\mathcal{O}(n\log^{3}n)
time with MMD error that is worse by at most a factor of
4
. Hence, KT-Compress++ can be practically deployed even for very large input sizes.

8.1
Related work on MMD coresets

While
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},o_{p}(n^{-\frac{1}{4}}))
-MMD coresets have been developed for specific
(\mathbb{P},{\mathbf{k}_{\star}})
pairings like the uniform distribution on the unit cube paired with a Sobolev kernel
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
, to the best of our knowledge, no prior
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},o_{p}(n^{-\frac{1}{4}}))
-MMD coreset constructions were known for the range of
\mathbb{P}
and
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
studied in this work.
For comparison, we review here both lower bounds and prior strategies for generating
coresets with small MMD.

Lower bounds

For any bounded and radial (i.e.,
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(x,y)\!=\!\kappa(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x\!-\!y}}}\right\|_{2}^{2})
) kernel
satisfying mild decay and smoothness conditions,

Phillips and Tai 2020
showed that any procedure outputting coresets of size
n^{\frac{1}{2}}
must suffer
\Omega(\min(\sqrt{d}n^{-\frac{1}{2}},n^{-\frac{1}{4}}))

\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}
for some (discrete) target distribution
\mathbb{P}
.
This lower bound applies, for example, to Matérn kernels and to infinitely smooth Gaussian kernels.
For any continuous and shift-invariant (i.e.,
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(x,y)\!=\!\kappa(x\!-\!y)
) kernel taking on at least two values,

Tolstikhin et al. 2017

showed that
any
estimator (including non-coreset estimators) based only on
n
i.i.d. draws from
\mathbb{P}
must suffer
C_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}n^{-\frac{1}{2}}

\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}
with probability at least
1/4
for some discrete target
\mathbb{P}
and a constant
C_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}
depending only
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
.
If, in addition,
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
is characteristic (i.e.,
\mmd_{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(\mu,\nu)\neq 0
when
\mu\!\neq\!\nu
), then
Tolstikhin et al. 2017
establish the same lower bound for some continuous target
\mathbb{P}
with infinitely differentiable density.
These last two lower bounds hold, for example, for Gaussian, Matérn, and B-spline kernels and apply in particular to any thinning algorithm that compresses
n
i.i.d. sample points without additional knowledge of
\mathbb{P}
.
For light-tailed
\mathbb{P}
and
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
, the kernel thinning guarantees of
Theorem

1
match each of these lower bounds up to factors of
\sqrt{\log n}
and constants depending on
d
.

Order
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},n^{-\frac{1}{4}})
-MMD coresets for general target
\mathbb{P}

By Prop. A.1 of
Tolstikhin et al. 2017
, an i.i.d. sample from
\mathbb{P}
yields an order
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},n^{-\frac{1}{4}})
-MMD coreset in probability.
Chen et al. 2010
showed that
kernel herding
with a finite-dimensional kernel (like the linear
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(x,y)=\langle{x},{y}\rangle
) finds an
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},(C_{\mathbb{P},{\mathbf{k}_{\star}},d}n)^{-\frac{1}{2}})
-MMD coreset for an inexplicit parameter
C_{\mathbb{P},{\mathbf{k}_{\star}},d}
. However,
Bach et al. 2012
showed that their analysis does not apply to any infinite-dimensional kernel (like the Gaussian, Matérn, and B-spline kernels studied in this work), as
C_{\mathbb{P},{\mathbf{k}_{\star}},d}
would necessarily equal
0
. The best known rate for kernel herding with bounded infinite-dimensional kernels
(
Lacoste-Julien et al. 2015
, Thm. G.1)
guarantees an order
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},n^{-\frac{1}{4}})
-MMD coreset, matching the i.i.d. guarantee.
For bounded kernels, the same guarantee is available for
Stein Point MCMC

(
Chen et al. 2019
, Thm. 1)
which greedily minimizes MMD
5
5

5

To bound
\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}
using
Chen et al. 2019
, choose
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{0}(x,y)={\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(x,y)-\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(x)-\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(y)+\mathbb{P}\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
.
over random draws from
\mathbb{P}
and for a variant of the
greedy sign selection
algorithm described in
Karnin and Liberty 2019
.
6
6

6

The statement of
Karnin and Liberty 2019
bounds
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\cdot}}}\right\|_{\infty}
, but the proof bounds
\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}
.

Slightly inferior guarantees were established for Stein points
(
Chen et al. 2018
, Thm. 1)
and Stein thinning
(
Riabiz et al. 2021
, Thm. 1)
, both of which accommodate unbounded kernels as well.

Finite-dimensional kernels

Harvey and Samadi 2014
construct
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},\sqrt{d}n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log^{2.5}n)
-MMD coresets for finite-dimensional linear kernels on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
but do not address infinite-dimensional kernels.

Uniform distribution on
[0,1]^{d}

The explicit low discrepancy
quasi-Monte Carlo (QMC)
construction of

Chen and Skriganov 2002

provides a
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},\mathcal{O}_{d}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log^{\frac{d-1}{2}}n))
-MMD coreset for an
L^{2}
discrepancy kernel when
\mathbb{P}
is the uniform distribution on the unit cube
[0,1]^{d}
. For the same target, the
online Haar strategy
of
Dwivedi et al. 2019
yields an

(n^{\frac{1}{2}},\mathcal{O}_{d}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log^{2d}n))
-MMD coreset in probability.
Dwivedi et al. 2019
also conjecture that a greedy variant of their Haar strategy would provide an improved
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},\mathcal{O}_{d}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log^{d}n))
-MMD coreset.
These constructions satisfy our quality criteria but are tailored specifically to the uniform distribution on the unit cube.

Unknown coreset quality

On compact manifolds, optimal coresets of size
n^{\frac{1}{2}}
minimize the weighted Riesz energy (a form of relative MMD with a weighted Riesz kernel) at known rates
(
Borodachov et al. 2014
)
; however, practical
minimum Riesz energy

(
Borodachov et al. 2014
)
and
minimum energy design

(
Joseph et al. 2015
;
Joseph et al. 2019
)
constructions have not been analyzed.
When
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
is nonnegative and the kernel matrix
({\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(x_{i},x_{j}))_{i,j=1}^{n}
satisfies a strong diagonal dominance condition,
Kim et al. 2016
show that greedy optimization of
\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}
yields an
MMD-critic
coreset
\hat{\mathcal{S}}
of size
n^{\frac{1}{2}}
satisfying

\textstyle\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n},\hat{\mathcal{S}})\leq(1-\frac{1}{e})\mmd_{\star}^{2}+\frac{1}{e}\mathbb{P}_{n}\mathbb{P}_{n}{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}\quad\text{for}\quad\mmd_{\star}=\min_{|\mathcal{S}|=\sqrt{n}}\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathbb{P}_{n},\mathcal{S}).

(24)

In the usual case when
\mathbb{P}_{n}\mathbb{P}_{n}{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}=\Omega(1)
, this error bound does not decay to
0
with
n
.

Paige et al. 2016
analyze the impact of approximating a kernel in
super-sampling with a reservoir
but do not analyze the quality of the constructed MMD coreset.
For the conditionally positive definite energy distance kernel,
Mak and Joseph 2018
establish that an optimal coreset of size
n^{\frac{1}{2}}
has
o(n^{-\frac{1}{4}})
MMD but do not provide a construction; in addition,
Mak and Joseph 2018
propose two
support points convex-concave procedures
for constructing MMD coresets but do not establish their optimality and do not analyze their quality.

8.2
Related work on weighted MMD coresets

While coresets satisfy a number of valuable constraints that are critical for some downstream applications—exact approximation of constants, automatic preservation of convex integrand constraints, compatibility with unweighted downstream tasks, easy visualization, straightforward sampling, and increased numerical stability against errors in integral evaluations
(
Karvonen et al. 2019
)
—some applications also support
weighted coreset
approximations of
\mathbb{P}
of the form
\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}w_{i}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{x_{i}}
for weights
w_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
that need not be equal, need not be nonnegative, or need not sum to
1
.
Notably, weighted coresets that depend on
\mathbb{P}
only through an i.i.d. sample of size
n
are subject to the same
\Omega(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})
MMD lower bounds of
Tolstikhin et al. 2017
described in
Section

8.1
.
Any constructions that violate these bounds do so only by exploiting additional information about
\mathbb{P}
(for example, exact knowledge of
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
) that is not generally available and not required for our kernel thinning guarantees.
Moreover, while weighted coresets need not provide satisfactory solutions to the unweighted coreset problem studied in this work, kernel thinning coreset points can be converted into an optimally weighted coreset of no worse quality by explicitly minimizing
\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathbb{P}_{n},\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}w_{i}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{x_{i}})
or, if computable,
\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathbb{P},\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}w_{i}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{x_{i}})
over the weights
w_{i}
in
\mathcal{O}(n^{3/2})
time.

With this context, we now review known weighted MMD coreset guarantees.
We highlight that
only one of the weighted
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},o(n^{-\frac{1}{4}}))
-MMD guarantees covers the unbounded distributions addressed in this work and that the single unbounded guarantee relies on a restrictive uniformly bounded eigenfunction assumption that is typically not satisfied.
In other words, our analysis establishes MMD improvements for practical
({\mathbf{k}_{\star}},\mathbb{P})
pairings not covered by prior weighted analyses.

\mathbb{P}
with bounded support

If the target
\mathbb{P}
has bounded density and bounded, regular support and
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
is a Gaussian or Matérn kernel, then
Bayesian quadrature

(
O’Hagan 1991
)
and
Bayes-Sard cubature

(
Karvonen et al. 2018
)
with quasi-uniform unisolvent point sets yield weighted
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},o(n^{-\frac{1}{4}}))
-MMD coresets
by
Wendland 2004
.
If
\mathbb{P}
has bounded support, and
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
has more than
d
continuous derivatives, then the
P-greedy
algorithm
(
De Marchi et al. 2005
)
also yields weighted
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},o(n^{-\frac{1}{4}}))
-MMD coresets by
Santin and Haasdonk 2017
.
For
({\mathbf{k}_{\star}},\mathbb{P})
pairs with compact support and sufficiently rapid eigenvalue decay, approximate
continuous volume sampling kernel quadrature

(
Belhadji et al. 2020
)
using the Gibbs sampler of
Rezaei and Gharan 2019
yields weighted coresets with
o(n^{-\frac{1}{4}})
root mean squared MMD.

Finite-dimensional kernels with compactly supported
\mathbb{P}

For compactly supported
\mathbb{P}
,

Briol et al. 2015
and

Bach et al. 2012

show that
Frank-Wolfe Bayesian quadrature
and weighted variants of kernel herding respectively yield weighted
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},o(n^{-\frac{1}{4}}))
-MMD coresets for continuous finite-dimensional kernels, but, by
Bach et al. 2012
, these analyses do not extend to infinite-dimensional kernels, like the Gaussian, Matérn, and B-spline kernels studied in this work.

Eigenfunction restrictions

For
({\mathbf{k}_{\star}},\mathbb{P})
pairs with known Mercer eigenfunctions,
Belhadji et al. 2019
bound the expected squared MMD of
determinantal point process (DPP) kernel quadrature
in terms of kernel eigenvalue decay and provide explicit rates for univariate Gaussian
\mathbb{P}
and uniform
\mathbb{P}
on
[0,1]
.
Their construction makes explicit use of the kernel eigenfunctions which are not available for most
({\mathbf{k}_{\star}},\mathbb{P})
pairings.
For
({\mathbf{k}_{\star}},\mathbb{P})
pairs with
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}={\boldsymbol{0}}
, uniformly bounded eigenfunctions, and rapidly decaying eigenvalues,
Liu and Lee 2017
prove that
black-box importance sampling
generates probability-weighted coresets with
o(n^{-\frac{1}{4}})
root mean squared MMD
but do not provide any examples verifying their assumptions.
The uniformly bounded eigenfunction condition is considered particularly difficult to check
(
Steinwart and Scovel 2012
)
, does not hold for Gaussian kernels with Gaussian
\mathbb{P}

(
Minh 2010
, Thm. 1)
, and need not hold even for infinitely univariate smooth kernels on
[0,1]

(
Zhou 2002
, Ex. 1)
.

Unknown coreset quality

Huszár and Duvenaud 2012
bound the MMD error of weighted
sequential Bayesian quadrature
coresets using weak submodularity, but this bound does not decay to zero with
n
.

Khanna and Mahoney 2019
prove that
weighted kernel herding
yields a weighted
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},\exp(-n^{\frac{1}{2}}/\kappa_{n}))
-MMD coreset.
However, the
\kappa_{n}
term in
Khanna and Mahoney 2019
is at least as large as the condition number of an
\sqrt{n}\times\sqrt{n}
kernel matrix, which for typical kernels (including the Gaussian and Matérn kernels) is
\Omega(\sqrt{n})

(
Koltchinskii and Giné 2000
;
El Karoui 2010
)
; the resulting MMD error bound therefore does not decay with
n
. The
ProtoGreedy
and
ProtoDash
algorithms of
Gurumoorthy et al. 2019
yield nonnegative weighted coresets
\hat{\mathcal{S}}
of size
n^{\frac{1}{2}}
satisfying

\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n},\hat{\mathcal{S}})\leq\mmd_{\star}^{2}+(\mathbb{P}_{n}\mathbb{P}_{n}{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}-\mmd_{\star}^{2})e^{-\lambda_{\sqrt{n}}}

where
\mmd_{\star}
is the optimal MMD error to
\mathbb{P}_{n}
for a nonnegatively weighted coreset of size
n^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
However, careful inspection reveals that
\lambda_{\sqrt{n}}\leq 1
for any kernel and any
n
.
Hence, in the usual case in which
\mathbb{P}_{n}\mathbb{P}_{n}{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}=\Omega(1)
, this error bound does not decay to
0
with
n
.

Campbell and Broderick 2019
prove that
Hilbert coresets via Frank-Wolfe
with
n
input points yield weighted order
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},\nu_{n}^{\sqrt{n}})
-MMD coresets for some
\nu_{n}<1
but do not analyze the dependence of
\nu_{n}
on
n
.

Non-MMD guarantees

For
\mathbb{P}
with continuously differentiable Lebesgue density and
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
a bounded Langevin Stein kernel with
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}={\boldsymbol{0}}
, Thm. 2 of
Oates et al. 2017
does not bound MMD but does prove that a randomized
control functionals
weighted coreset
satisfies
\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[(\mathbb{E}f-\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}w_{i}f(x_{i}))^{2}]}\leq{C_{\mathbb{P},{\mathbf{k}_{\star}},d,f}}{/n^{\frac{7}{24}}}
for each
f
in the RKHS of
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
and an unspecified
C_{\mathbb{P},{\mathbf{k}_{\star}},d,f}
.
This bound is asymptotically better than the
\Omega(n^{-\frac{1}{4}})
guarantee for unweighted i.i.d. coresets but worse than the unweighted kernel thinning guarantees of
Theorem

1
.
On compact domains, Thm. 1 of
Oates et al. 2019

establishes improved rates for the same weighted coreset when both
\mathbb{P}
and
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
are sufficiently smooth.

Bardenet and Hardy 2020
establish an
n^{-\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{4d}}
asymptotic decay of
\mathbb{E}f-\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}w_{i}f(x_{i})
for DPP kernel quadrature with
\mathbb{P}
on
[-1,1]^{d}
and each
f
in the RKHS of a particular kernel.

8.3
Related work on
L^{\infty}
coresets

A number of alternative strategies are available for constructing coresets with
L^{\infty}
guarantees.
For example, for any bounded
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
, Cauchy-Schwarz and the reproducing property imply that

\textstyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{(\mathbb{P}-\mathbb{P}_{n}){\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}}}\right\|_{\infty}=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(z,\cdot),\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}-\mathbb{P}_{n}{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}}\right\rangle_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}}}\right|\leq\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P}_{n})\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}}}\right\|_{\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}},

(25)

so that all of the order
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},n^{-\frac{1}{4}})
-MMD coreset constructions discussed in
Section

8.1
also yield order
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},n^{-\frac{1}{4}})
-
L^{\infty}
coresets.
However, none of those constructions is known to provide a
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},o(n^{-\frac{1}{4}}))
-
L^{\infty}
coreset.

A series of breakthroughs due to
Joshi et al. 2011
;
Phillips 2013
;
Phillips and Tai 2018
;
Phillips and Tai 2020
;
Tai 2020
has led to a sequence of increasingly compressed
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},o(n^{-\frac{1}{4}}))
-
L^{\infty}
coreset constructions, with the best known guarantees currently due to
Phillips and Tai 2020
and
Tai 2020
.
Given
n
input points,

Phillips and Tai 2020
developed an offline, polynomial-time construction to find an
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},\mathcal{O}_{p}(\sqrt{d}n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{\log n}))
-
L^{\infty}
coreset
for Lipschitz kernels exhibiting suitable decay, while
Tai 2020
developed an offline construction for Gaussian kernels that runs in
\Omega(d^{5d})
time and yields an
(n^{\frac{1}{2}},\mathcal{O}_{p}(2^{d}n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{\log(d\log n)}))
-
L^{\infty}
coreset. More details on these constructions based on the Gram-Schmidt walk of
Bansal et al. 2018
can be found in
Appendix

S
.
Notably, the Phillips and Tai (hereafter, PT) guarantee is tighter than that of
Theorem

4
by a factor of
\sqrt{\log\log n}
for sub-Gaussian kernels and input points and
\sqrt{\log n}
for heavy-tailed kernels and input points.
Similarly, the Tai guarantee provides an improvement when
n
is doubly-exponential in the dimension, that is, when
\sqrt{d\log n}=\Omega(2^{d})
.

Moreover, by
Theorem

2
, we may apply the PT and Tai constructions to a square-root kernel
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
to obtain comparable MMD guarantees for the target kernel
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
with high probability.
However, kernel thinning has a number of practical advantages that lead us to recommend it.
First with
n
input points, using standard matrix multiplication, the PT and Tai constructions have
\Omega(n^{4})
computational complexity and
\Omega(n^{2})
storage costs, a substantial increase over the
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
running time and
\mathcal{O}(n\min(d,n))
storage of kernel thinning.
Second,
kt-split
is an online algorithm while the PT and Tai constructions require the entire set of input points to be available a priori.
Finally, each halving round of
kt-split
splits the sample size exactly in half, allowing the user to run all
m
halving rounds simultaneously; the PT and Tai constructions require a rebalancing step after each round forcing the halving rounds to be conducted sequentially.

8.4
Future directions

Several other opportunities for future development recommend themselves.
First, since our results cover any target
\mathbb{P}
with at least
2d
moments—even discrete and other non-smooth targets—a natural question is whether tighter error bounds with better sample complexities are available when
\mathbb{P}
is also known to have a smooth Lebesgue density.
Second, the MMD to
L^{\infty}
reduction in
Theorem

2
applies also to weighted
L^{\infty}
coresets, and, in applications in which weighted point sets are supported, we would expect either quality or compression improvements from employing non-uniform weights
(
Turner et al. 2021
, see, e.g.)
.

Table of contents

Appendix A
Appendix Notation

For each
p\geq 1
, we define
{L}^{p}
as the set of measurable
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{g}}}\right\|_{L^{p}}\triangleq(\int|g(x)^{p}|dx)^{1/p}<\infty
and
C^{p}
as the set of
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
for which all partial derivatives of order
p
exist and are continuous.
For a kernel
\mathbf{k}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, we also write

\mathbf{k}\in{L}^{2,\infty}
to indicate that
\mathbf{k}
is measurable with finite

\textstyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\mathbf{k}}}}\right\|_{L^{2,\infty}}\triangleq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\int\mathbf{k}^{2}(x,y)dy}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\mathbf{k}(x,\cdot)}}}\right\|_{L^{2}}.

(26)

Throughout, we follow
the unitary angular frequency convention of
Wendland 2004
and define the Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}(f)
of an integrable complex function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
via

\textstyle\mathcal{F}({f})(\omega)\triangleq\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{{d}/{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)e^{-i\langle{x},{\omega}\rangle}dx\quad\text{for all}\quad\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(27)

Appendix B
Proof of
Proposition

1
: MMD guarantee for MCMC

By
Douc et al. 2018
, a homogeneous

\phi
-irreducible geometrically ergodic Markov chain with stationary distribution
\mathbb{P}
is also aperiodic with a unique stationary distribution.
7
7

7

In
Havet et al. 2020
;
Douc et al. 2018
the term
irreducible
is synonymous with
\phi
-irreducible as defined by
Gallegos-Herrada et al. 2023
.

Since
(x_{i})_{i=0}^{\infty}
are the iterates of such a chain,
there exist,
by
Gallegos-Herrada et al. 2023
,
constants
\rho\in(0,1)
and
\tau<\infty
and a measurable
\mathbb{P}
-almost everywhere finite function
V:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[1,\infty]

satisfying
\mathbb{P}V<\infty
and

\displaystyle\!\sup_{\text{measurable }h:\frac{|h(x)|}{V(x)}\leq 1,\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\mathbb{E}[h(x_{i})\mid x_{0}=x]-\mathbb{P}h|\leq\tau V(x)\rho^{i},\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ \text{and}\ i\in\mathbb{N}.

(28)

Since
V
is finite
\mathbb{P}
-almost everywhere, we will choose
c(x)=\infty\Leftrightarrow V(x)=\infty
to ensure that our claim is (vacuously) true whenever
V(x_{0})=\infty
.

Hereafter, suppose
V(x_{0})<\infty
.
Since the Markov chain is irreducible and aperiodic with a unique stationary distribution
\mathbb{P}
, Assump. H1 of
Havet et al. 2020
is satisfied.
Hence, by an application of
Havet et al. 2020
with

V^{\prime}=V\rho^{g}
and
\zeta=2/\rho^{g}
for sufficiently large
g\in\mathbb{N}
,
there exists a
set
C(x_{0})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
that contains
x_{0}
and satisfies Assumps. H2 and H3 of
Havet et al. 2020
.

Now fix any
y_{1},\dots,y_{n},z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We invoke the definition of MMD
Eq.

2
, the triangle inequality, the reproducing property of an RKHS
(
Steinwart and Christmann 2008
, Def. 4.18)
, and Cauchy-Schwarz in turn to deduce a bounded differences property for MMD:

\textstyle\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathbb{P},\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{y_{i}})-\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathbb{P},\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{\delta}_{z_{i}})

(29)

\textstyle=\sup_{\|{f}\|_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}\leq 1}|\mathbb{P}f-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(y_{i})|-\sup_{\|{f}\|_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}\leq 1}|\mathbb{P}f-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(z_{i})|

(30)

\textstyle\leq\sup_{\|{f}\|_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}\leq 1}|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(y_{i})-f(z_{i})|=\sup_{\|{f}\|_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}\leq 1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(y_{i},\cdot)-{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(z_{i},\cdot),f}}\right\rangle_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}|

(31)

\textstyle\leq\sup_{\|{f}\|_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}\leq 1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(y_{i},\cdot)-{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(z_{i},\cdot)}\|_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}\|{f}\|_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}

(32)

\textstyle=\sup_{\|{f}\|_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}\leq 1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(y_{i},y_{i})+{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(z_{i},z_{i})-2{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(y_{i},z_{i})}\|{f}\|_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}\leq\frac{2}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}}}\right\|_{\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{y_{i}\neq z_{i}}}}\right].

(33)

Since
x_{0}
belongs to a set
C(x_{0})
satisfying Assumps. H2 and H3 of
Havet et al. 2020
, McDiarmid’s inequality for geometrically ergodic Markov chains

(
Havet et al. 2020
, Thm. 3.1)

implies that,
with probability at least

1-\delta
conditional on
x_{0}
,

\textstyle\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P}_{n})\leq\mathbb{E}[\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P}_{n})\mid x_{0}]+\sqrt{c_{1}(x_{0})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}}}\right\|_{\infty}\log(1/\delta)/n}

(34)

where
c_{1}(x_{0})
is a finite value depending only on the transition probabilities of the chain and the set
C(x_{0})
.

Now, define the
\mathbb{P}
centered kernel
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{\mathbb{P}}(x,y)={\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(x,y)-\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(x)-\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(y)+\mathbb{P}\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
.
To bound the expectation, we will use a slight modification of Lem. 3 of
Riabiz et al. 2021
.
The original lemma used the assumption of
V
-uniform ergodicity
(
Meyn and Tweedie 2012
, Defn. (16.0.1))
and the assumption
V(x)\geq\sqrt{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{\mathbb{P}}(x,x)}
solely to argue that, for some
R>0
,

\textstyle|\mathbb{E}[f(x_{i})\mid x_{0}=x]-\mathbb{P}f|\leq RV(x)\rho^{i}\quad\text{for all}\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\quad\text{and}\quad f\in\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{\mathbb{P}}}\ \text{with}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{f}}}\right\|_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{\mathbb{P}}}=1.

(35)

In our case, since
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{\mathbb{P}}
is bounded and any
f\in\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{\mathbb{P}}}
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{f}}}\right\|_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{\mathbb{P}}}=1
satisfies

\textstyle|f(x)|=|\langle{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{\mathbb{P}}(x,\cdot)},{f}\rangle_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{\mathbb{P}}}|\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{\mathbb{P}}(x,\cdot)}}}\right\|_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{\mathbb{P}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{f}}}\right\|_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{\mathbb{P}}}=\sqrt{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{\mathbb{P}}(x,x)}\leq\sqrt{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{\mathbb{P}}}}}\right\|_{\infty}}\ \text{for all}\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(36)

by the reproducing property and Cauchy-Schwarz, the geometric ergodicity property
Eq.

57
implies the analogous bound

\textstyle|\mathbb{E}[f(x_{i})\mid x_{0}=x]-\mathbb{P}f|\leq\tau\sqrt{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{\mathbb{P}}}}}\right\|_{\infty}}V(x)\rho^{i}\ \text{for all}\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ \text{and}\ f\in\mathcal{H}_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{\mathbb{P}}}\ \text{with}\ \mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{f}}}\right\|_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{\mathbb{P}}}=1.

(37)

Hence, the conclusions of
Riabiz et al. 2021
with
R=\tau\sqrt{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{\mathbb{P}}}}}\right\|_{\infty}}
hold under our assumptions.
Jensen’s inequality and the conclusion of Lem. 3 of
Riabiz et al. 2021
now yield the sure bound

\textstyle\mathbb{E}[

\textstyle\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P}_{n})\mid x_{0}]^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}[\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P}_{n})^{2}\mid x_{0}]

(38)

\textstyle=\mathbb{E}[\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{\mathbb{P}}(x_{i},x_{i})+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j\neq i}{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{\mathbb{P}}(x_{i},x_{j})\mid x_{0}]

(39)

\textstyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}_{\mathbb{P}}}}}\right\|_{\infty}(1+\frac{2\tau\rho}{1-\rho}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathbb{E}[V(x_{i})\mid x_{0}])\leq\frac{4}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}}}\right\|_{\infty}(1+\frac{2\tau\rho}{1-\rho}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathbb{E}[V(x_{i})\mid x_{0}]).

(40)

Now, define
c_{2}(x_{0})\triangleq\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathbb{E}[V(x_{i})\mid x_{0}]
.
Since
V(x_{0})<\infty
, the geometric ergodicity property
Eq.

57
and the fact that
\mathbb{P}V<\infty
imply

\textstyle c_{2}(x_{0})\leq\mathbb{P}V+V(x_{0})\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\textstyle\frac{1}{n}}{\textstyle\sum}_{i=1}^{n-1}\rho^{i}\leq\mathbb{P}V+V(x_{0})\rho<\infty.

(41)

Taking
c(x_{0})=\sqrt{2}\max(c_{1}(x_{0}),4c_{2}(x_{0})(1+\frac{2\tau\rho}{1-\rho}))
completes the proof.

Appendix C
Proof of
Proposition

2
:
Proposition 2

(Almost sure radius growth)
.

We prove this result for the identically distributed case in
Section

C.1
and for the Markov chain case in
Section

C.2
.

C.1
Radius growth for identically distributed sequences

Suppose
x_{0}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
are drawn identically from
\mathbb{P}
.
Claim
Item

(a)
is true by definition.
To establish the remaining claims,
we use the following more general result proved in
Section

C.1.1
.

Lemma 1

(Growth rate for identically distributed sequence)
.

Consider a sequence of identically distributed random variables
(Y_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}
and a measurable function
\psi:\real\to\real
with an increasing inverse function
\psi^{-1}
. If
\psi(Y_{1})\geq 0
almost surely, then

\textstyle\mathrm{Pr}(\max_{i\leq n}Y_{i}>\psi^{-1}(n)\ \text{for some}\ n\in\mathbb{N})\leq\mathbb{E}(\psi(Y_{1})).

(42)

Consequently, if
\mathbb{E}(\psi(Y_{1}))<\infty
, then, for any
\delta\in(0,1]
,

\textstyle\mathrm{Pr}(\max_{i\leq n}Y_{i}\leq\psi^{-1}(\frac{n\mathbb{E}[\psi(Y_{1})]}{\delta})\ \text{for all}\ n\in\mathbb{N})\geq 1-\delta.

(43)

Fix any
\delta\in(0,1]
.

Lemma

1
with
Y_{i}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x_{i}}}}\right\|_{2}

implies that, with probability
1-\delta
,

\textstyle R_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}\leq\psi^{-1}(\frac{n\mathbb{E}[\psi(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x_{1}}}}\right\|_{2})]}{\delta})\ \text{for all}\ n\in\mathbb{N}

(44)

for
\psi^{-1}(r)=\frac{\sqrt{\log r}}{\sqrt{c}}
in case
Item

(b)
,
\psi^{-1}(r)=\frac{\log r}{c}
in case
Item

(c)
, and
\psi^{-1}(r)=r^{1/\rho}
in case
Item

(d)
.
As a result we have, with probability
1-\delta
,
R_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}=\mathcal{O}_{d}(\sqrt{\log n})
in case
Item

(b)
,
R_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}=\mathcal{O}_{d}(\log n)
in case
Item

(c)
, and
R_{\mathcal{S}_{n}}=\mathcal{O}_{d}(n^{1/\rho})
in case
Item

(d)
.
Since
\delta
is arbitrary, these orders hold with probability
1
as claimed.

C.1.1
Proof of
Lemma

1
:
Lemma 1

(Growth rate for identically distributed sequence)
.

We make use of three lemmas.
The first rewrites maximum exceedance events in terms of individual variable exceedance events when thresholds are nondecreasing.

Lemma 2

(Exceedance equivalence)
.

For any real-valued
(a_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
and nondecreasing
(b_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
,

\textstyle\max_{i\leq n}a_{i}>b_{n}\ \text{for some}\ n\in\mathbb{N}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad a_{i}>b_{i}\ \text{for some}\ i\in\mathbb{N}.

(45)

Proof

The
\Leftarrow
part follows immediately. To prove the
\Rightarrow
part, suppose
\max_{i\leq n^{\star}}a_{i}>b_{n^{\star}}
for some
n^{\star}
. Then there exists an
i\leq n^{\star}
with
a_{i}>b_{n^{\star}}\geq b_{i}
since
(b_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
is nondecreasing.

The second bounds the probability of growth rate violation for
any
sequence of random variables in terms of a sum of exceedance probabilities.

Lemma 3

(Growth rate for arbitrary sequence)
.

For any sequence of random variables
(Y_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}
and
a nondecreasing real-valued sequence
(b_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
, we have

\textstyle\mathrm{Pr}(\max_{i\leq n}Y_{i}>b_{n}\ \text{for some}\ n\in\mathbb{N})=\mathrm{Pr}(Y_{i}>b_{i}\ \text{for some}\ i\in\mathbb{N})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{Pr}(Y_{i}>b_{i}).

(46)

Proof

The result follows from immediately from
Lemma

2
and the union bound.

The third lemma bounds a sum of exceedance probabilities whenever the random variables are identically distributed and nonnegative.

Lemma 4

(Bounding exceedances with expectations)
.

If the random variables
(Z_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
are identically distributed and almost surely nonnegative, then

\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{Pr}(Z_{i}>i)\leq\mathbb{E}(Z_{1}).

(47)

Proof

Since
Z_{1}
is almost surely nonnegative, we have
Z_{1}=\int_{0}^{Z_{1}}dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{I}(Z_{1}>t)dt
almost surely.
Tonelli’s theorem
(
Mukherjea 1972
, Thm. 1)
therefore implies that

\textstyle\mathbb{E}(Z_{1})

\textstyle=\mathbb{E}(\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{I}(Z_{1}>t)dt)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Pr}(Z_{1}>t)dt

(48)

\textstyle\geq\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Pr}(Z_{1}>\lceil{t}\rceil)dt=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{Pr}(Z_{1}>i)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{Pr}(Z_{i}>i)

(49)

where the final inequality uses the identically distributed assumption.

Since
\psi(Y_{1})\geq 0
almost surely and
\psi^{-1}

is increasing, we invoke

Lemma

4
with
Z_{i}=\psi(Y_{i})
,
the invertibility of
\psi
, and

Lemma

3
with
b_{i}=\psi^{-1}(i)
in turn to conclude

\textstyle\mathbb{E}(\psi(Y_{1}))

\textstyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:tailsum_bound_expectation}}}{{\geq}}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{Pr}(\psi(Y_{i})>i)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{Pr}(Y_{i}>\psi^{-1}(i))

(50)

\textstyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:max_equivalence}}}{{\geq}}\mathrm{Pr}(\max_{i\leq n}Y_{i}>\psi^{-1}(n)\ \text{for some}\ n\in\mathbb{N}).

(51)

C.2
Radius growth for MCMC

Now suppose

x_{0}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
are the iterates of a homogeneous
\phi
-irreducible geometrically ergodic Markov chain
with initial state
x_{0}
, subsequent iterates
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
, and stationary distribution
\mathbb{P}
.
Our claims will follow from the following more detailed result proved in
Section

C.2.1
.

Lemma 5

(Growth rate for MCMC)
.

Consider a homogeneous
\phi
-irreducible geometrically ergodic Markov chain
with initial state
x_{0}
, subsequent iterates
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
, and stationary distribution
\mathbb{P}
.
There exist constants
\rho\in(0,1)
and
\tau<\infty
and a measurable
\mathbb{P}
-almost everywhere finite function
V:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[1,\infty]
such that, for any index
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
measurable function
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, measurable nonnegative function
\psi
on
\mathbb{R}
with increasing inverse function
\psi^{-1}
,
and
X\sim\mathbb{P}
,

\textstyle\mathrm{Pr}(\max_{i\leq n}g(x_{i})>\psi^{-1}(n)\ \text{for some}\ n\in\mathbb{N}\mid x_{0})\leq

(52)

\textstyle\mathbb{E}(\psi(g(X)))+{\textstyle\frac{\tau\rho^{j+1}}{1-\rho}}V(x_{0})+\sum_{i=1}^{j}\mathrm{Pr}(g(x_{i})>\psi^{-1}(i)\mid x_{0}).

(53)

Now suppose
V(x_{0})<\infty
, and fix any measurable nonnegative

\psi
on
\mathbb{R}
with increasing
\psi^{-1}
and any measurable
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
.
If
\mathbb{E}(\psi(g(X)))<\infty
, then, for any
\delta\in(0,1]
, there exists a constant
c_{\delta,\psi\circ g}(x_{0})\in(0,\infty)
such that

\textstyle\mathrm{Pr}(\max_{i\leq n}g(x_{i})\leq\psi^{-1}(c_{\delta,\psi\circ g}(x_{0})n)\ \text{for all}\ n\in\mathbb{N}\mid x_{0})\geq 1-\delta.

(54)

Moreover, if
\mathbb{P}
is compactly supported, then for any
\delta\in(0,1]
, there exists a constant
c_{\delta}(x_{0})\in(0,\infty)
such that

\textstyle\mathrm{Pr}(\sup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x_{i}}}}\right\|_{2}\leq c_{\delta}(x_{0})\mid x_{0})\geq 1-\delta.

(55)

Instantiate the function
V
from
Lemma

5
, and suppose that
V(x_{0})<\infty
, an event that holds for
\mathbb{P}
-almost every
x_{0}
.
Claims
Items

(b)
,
(c)
, and
(d)
then follow by invoking the time-uniform tail bound
Eq.

54
with
g=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{\cdot}}}\right\|_{2}
and proceeding as in
Section

C.1
. Finally, claim
Item

(a)
follows from the bound
Eq.

55
, which establishes
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x_{i}}}}\right\|_{2}=\mathcal{O}_{d}(1)
with probability
1
conditional on
x_{0}
.

C.2.1
Proof of
Lemma

5
:
Lemma 5

(Growth rate for MCMC)
.

The proof closely parallels that of
Lemma

1
except that we substitute the following estimate for
Lemma

4
.

Lemma 6

(Bounding MCMC exceedances with expectations)
.

Consider a homogeneous
\phi
-irreducible geometrically ergodic Markov chain
with initial state
x_{0}
, subsequent iterates
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
, and stationary distribution
\mathbb{P}
.
There exist constants
\rho\in(0,1)
and
\tau<\infty
and a measurable
\mathbb{P}
-almost everywhere finite function
V:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[1,\infty]
such that, for any index
j\in\mathbb{N}
, measurable nonnegative function
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
X\sim\mathbb{P}
,

\textstyle\sum_{i=j}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(f(x_{i})>i\mid x_{0})\leq\mathbb{E}(f(X))+\frac{\tau\rho^{j}}{1-\rho}V(x_{0}).

(56)

Proof

By
Douc et al. 2018
, a homogeneous

\phi
-irreducible geometrically ergodic Markov chain with stationary distribution
\mathbb{P}
is also aperiodic.
By
Gallegos-Herrada et al. 2023
, there exist constants
\rho\in(0,1)
and
\tau<\infty
and a measurable
\mathbb{P}
-almost everywhere finite function
V:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[1,\infty]
satisfying

\displaystyle\sup_{h:|h(x)|\leq V(x),\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\mathbb{E}[h(x_{i})\mid x_{0}=x]-\mathbb{P}h|\leq\tau V(x)\rho^{i}\quad\text{for all}\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(57)

Applying this result to the functions
h_{i}(x)\triangleq\mathbb{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{f(x)>i}}}\right]
, we find that

\textstyle\sum_{i=j}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(f(x_{i})>i\mid x_{0})\leq\sum_{i=j}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(f(X)>i)+\sum_{i=j}^{\infty}\tau V(x_{0})\rho^{i}\leq\mathbb{E}[f(X)]+\frac{\tau\rho^{j}}{1-\rho}V(x_{0})

(58)

where the final inequality uses
Lemma

4
.

Fix any
V
,
\rho
, and
\tau
satisfying the conclusions of
Lemma

6
, any measurable
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, and any measurable nonnegative

\psi
on
\mathbb{R}
with increasing
\psi^{-1}
.
The first claim
Eq.

52
follows by applying
Lemma

3
with
b_{i}=\psi^{-1}(i)
, the assumed invertiblity and strict monotonicity of
\psi^{-1}
, and
Lemma

6
with
f=\psi\circ g
in turn to find that

\textstyle\mathbb{P}(\max_{i\leq n}g(x_{i})>\psi^{-1}(n)\ \text{for some}\ n\mid x_{0})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(g(x_{i})>\psi^{-1}(i)\mid x_{0})

(59)

\textstyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(\psi(g(x_{i}))>i\mid x_{0})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{j}\mathbb{P}(\psi(g(x_{i}))>i\mid x_{0})+\mathbb{E}(\psi(g(X)))+{\textstyle\frac{\tau\rho^{j+1}}{1-\rho}}V(x_{0}).

(60)

Now suppose
V(x_{0})<\infty
, fix any
\delta\in(0,1]
, and let
j
be the smallest positive index with
{\textstyle\frac{\tau\rho^{j+1}}{1-\rho}}V(x_{0})<\frac{\delta}{3}
.
Since each
\psi(g(x_{i}))
is a tight random variable given
x_{0}
, we can additionally choose a constant
c_{\delta,\psi\circ g}(x_{0})\in(0,\infty)
satisfying
\sum_{i=1}^{j}\mathbb{P}(\psi(g(x_{i}))/c_{\delta,\psi\circ g}(x_{0})>i\mid x_{0})<\frac{\delta}{3}
and
\mathbb{E}(\psi(g(X)))/c_{\delta,\psi\circ g}(x_{0})<\frac{\delta}{3}
. The claim
Eq.

54
now follows by applying the initial result
Eq.

52
to the function
\psi/c_{\delta,\psi\circ g}(x_{0})
.

To establish the final claim
Eq.

55
,
suppose that
\mathbb{P}
is compactly supported.
Since
\mathbb{P}
has compact support and each
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x_{i}}}}\right\|_{2}
is a tight random variable, there exists a constant
c_{\delta}\in(0,\infty)
satisfying
\mathrm{Pr}_{X\sim\mathbb{P}}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{X}}}\right\|_{2}>c_{\delta})=0
and
\sum_{i=1}^{j}\mathbb{P}(\psi(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x_{i}}}}\right\|_{2})>c_{\delta}\mid x_{0})<\frac{\delta}{2}
.
The union bound and the geometric ergodicity property
Eq.

57
applied to the function
h(x)=\mathbb{I}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x}}}\right\|_{2}>c_{\delta})
with
\mathbb{P}h=0
now imply

\textstyle\mathrm{Pr}(\sup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x_{i}}}}\right\|_{2}>c_{\delta}\mid x_{0})

\textstyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{Pr}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x_{i}}}}\right\|_{2}>c_{\delta}\mid x_{0})

(61)

\textstyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{j}\mathrm{Pr}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x_{i}}}}\right\|_{2}>c_{\delta}\mid x_{0})+\tau V(x_{0})\sum_{i=j+1}\rho^{i}

(62)

\textstyle=\sum_{i=1}^{j}\mathrm{Pr}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\|{x_{i}}}}\right\|_{2}>c_{\delta}\mid x_{0})+\frac{\tau\rho^{j+1}}{1-\rho}V(x_{0})<\frac{\delta}{2}+\frac{\delta}{3}<\delta.

(63)

Appendix D
Proof of
Proposition

3
: Shift-invariant square-root kernels

Bochner’s theorem
(
Bochner 1933
;
Wendland 2004
, Thm. 6.6)
implies that

{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
is a kernel since
{\kappa}_{\mathrm{rt}}
is the Fourier transform of a finite Borel measure with Lebesgue density
\sqrt{\widehat{\kappa}}
.
Moreover, as

{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}(x,\cdot)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/4}}\mathcal{F}(e^{-i\langle{\cdot},{x}\rangle}\sqrt{\widehat{\kappa}})
and
e^{-i\langle{\cdot},{x}\rangle}\sqrt{\widehat{\kappa}}
is integrable and square integrable,
the Plancherel-Parseval identity
(
Wendland 2004
, Proof of Thm. 5.23)
implies that

\textstyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}(x,z){\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}(y,z)dz

\textstyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/4}}e^{-i\langle{\omega},{x}\rangle}\sqrt{\widehat{\kappa}(\omega)}\ \frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/4}}e^{i\langle{\omega},{y}\rangle}\sqrt{\widehat{\kappa}(\omega)}d\omega

(64)

\textstyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-i\langle{\omega},{x-y}\rangle}\widehat{\kappa}(\omega)d\omega={\mathbf{k}_{\star}}(x,y)

(65)

confirming that
{\mathbf{k}}_{\mathrm{rt}}
is a square-root kernel of
{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}
.

Appendix E
Proof of
Theorem

1
: MMD guarantee for kernel thinning

By design,
kt-swap
ensures

\textstyle\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathcal{S}_{n},\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{KT}})\leq\mmd_{{\mathbf{k}_{\star}}}(\mathcal{S}_{n},\mathcal{S}^{(m,1)}),

(66)

where
\mathcal{S}^{(m,1)}
denotes the first coreset returned by
kt-split
. Next, applying
Corollary

5
, in particular, the bound
Eq.

259
yields the desired claim.

Appendix F
Proof of
Remark

4
: Finite-time and anytime guarantees

We prove the three claims one by one.

Finite time guarantee

For the case with known
n
, the claim follows simply by noting that

\textstyle\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{2^{j-1}}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{2^{m-j}\lfloor{n/2^{m}}\rfloor}\frac{\delta}{n}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{2^{m}}{m}\lfloor{\frac{n}{2^{m}}}\rfloor\frac{\delta}{2n}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{2^{m}}{m}\frac{n}{2^{m}}\frac{\delta}{2n}=\frac{\delta}{2}.

(67)

Any time guarantee

When the input size
n
is not known in advance but is chosen independently of the randomness in kernel thinning,
we first note that

\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(i+1)\log^{2}(i+1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}2,\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{j=1}^{m}2^{j}=2^{m+1}-2\leq 2^{m+1}.

(68)

where step (i) can be verified using mathematical programming software.
Therefore, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, with
\delta_{i}=\frac{m\delta}{2^{m+2}(i+1)\log^{2}(i+1)}
, we have

\textstyle\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{2^{j-1}}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{2^{m-j}\lfloor{n/2^{m}}\rfloor}\delta_{i}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{2^{j-1}}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\delta_{i}

\textstyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{2^{j-1}}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{m\delta}{2^{m+2}(i+1)\log^{2}(i+1)}

(69)

\textstyle=\frac{\delta}{2^{m+3}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}2^{j}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(i+1)\log^{2}(i+1)}}}\right)

(70)
